**Physical Description of the Manuscript**

The image captures the <term>fore-edge</term> <note>the edge of the pages opposite the spine</note> of this historical manuscript. The <term>binding</term> is composed of <term>vellum</term> <note>a high-quality writing surface or binding material made from specially prepared calfskin</note>. The cover edges are folded over the text block, a common feature in historical "limp vellum" bindings which were both durable and relatively flexible. The visible waviness of the pages is a natural characteristic of parchment or vellum reacting to changes in humidity over centuries.

->A twelve-line poem by Pierre de la Porte of Montreuil to the<-
## R E A D E R.

Receive this little book that offers lessons on things divine;
Take up Hermes, a man of ancient faith.
Hermes, whom Thracian Orpheus could not match,
Nor Linus, the child of Calliope.
<note>Linus was a legendary Greek musician; Calliope was the Muse of epic poetry.</note>
He surpasses Zalmoxis and the Athenian Eumolpus,
<note>Zalmoxis was a legendary social and religious reformer of the Getae; Eumolpus was the mythical founder of the Eleusinian Mysteries in Athens.</note>
Whom ancient fame confirms as divine oracles.
This book is useful for those fleeing the world's shadow,
For whom the true, pure lights of radiance are pleasing.
Here you will learn what wisdom is, and supreme power;
Here you will be able to learn the will of the highest God.
Furthermore, you will find the Mixing-Bowl filled with
Nectared liquid: which you, reader, may buy for a pittance.

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page is the formal dedication of the volume. It follows the title page and table of contents. The author, Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples (a leading French humanist and theologian), is addressing his patron, Guillaume Briçonnet, who was a significant figure in the French "Prereform" movement and a protector of humanist scholars.</meta>

## TO THE HOLY PRELATE GUILLAUME BRIÇONNET, BISHOP OF LODÈVE, JACQUES LEFÈVRE SENDS GREETINGS.
<note>Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples (c. 1455–1536), also known by his Latin name Jacobus Faber Stapulensis, was a pioneer of Greek studies in France and sought to reform the Church through a return to ancient texts.</note>

<margin>v</margin>
The entire collection of numbers is divided into the even and the odd, so that no number can be found that is not either even or odd—though the nature of the odd is more divine. <note>Lefèvre is using a Pythagorean/Neoplatonic metaphor here: in ancient number theory, "odd" numbers were often associated with the limited, the male, and the divine, while "even" numbers were associated with the infinite or material world.</note> In the same way, the whole of mortal life is divided into business and leisure—into action and contemplation—but contemplation is the more divine. Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle agree with this sentiment; for them, business is for the sake of leisure, and action finds its purpose in contemplation—specifically that contemplation which, relating to wisdom, lifts our minds toward God. 

Therefore, since this is the highest goal of our life—to know God and to strive toward Him with "all sails set" (as the saying goes) and to run toward Him with our whole heart—I believed I would do something not unwelcome to your pious mind if I were to revise two small works of Hermes Trismegistus (a most ancient theologian): one on the wisdom and power of God, titled **PIMANDER**, and the other on the divine will, titled **ASCLEPIUS**, and if I were to provide arguments that might shed some light upon them. 

Having revised them and assisted them in this manner, I offer them to you, so that whenever you take a breath from business and turn toward leisure—as if emerging from the night into a clarity welcome to the eyes—your noble mind, weary from action, may find a moment of quiet contemplation and gather the nourishment of divine meditation. 

Furthermore, to the *Pimander* and *Asclepius* I have added the **MIXING-BOWL OF HERMES** <note>original: "Crater Hermetis"</note>, a work not ancient but modern, yet nonetheless pious. You will understand that I have done this all the more gladly because I know you never suffer from a satiety of divine matters. Farewell, our protector and the glory of prelates.

<summary>Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples dedicates his edition of Hermetic texts to Bishop Guillaume Briçonnet, arguing that the contemplative life and the study of ancient theology are the highest pursuits of the human soul.</summary>
<keywords>Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples, Guillaume Briçonnet, Hermes Trismegistus, Pimander, Asclepius, contemplation, Neoplatonism, theology, humanism</keywords>

<meta>This page contains the preface by Marsilio Ficino for his translation of the *Corpus Hermeticum*, dedicated to his patron Cosimo de' Medici. It establishes the legendary genealogy and divine status of Hermes Trismegistus.</meta>

# ¶ The Argument of Marsilio Ficino the Florentine: on the book of Mercury Trismegistus: to Cosimo de' Medici.

<margin>
Moses.
Atlas.
Mercury.
Augustine.
Cicero.
Lactantius.
Argus.
</margin>
At that time when Moses was born, there flourished Atlas the astrologer, brother of Prometheus the Natural Philosopher <term>Physici: a scholar of the natural world and its laws</term> and the maternal grandfather of the elder Mercury, whose grandson was Mercury Trismegistus. Augustine writes this concerning him; although Cicero and Lactantius maintain there were five Mercuries in succession, and that the fifth was he who was called *Theut* <note>original: *theut*, identifying Hermes with the Egyptian god Thoth</note> by the Egyptians, but *Trismegistus* <note>meaning 'Thrice-Greatest'</note> by the Greeks. They assert that this man killed Argus, ruled over the Egyptians, and handed down laws and letters to them. Indeed, he established the characters of their letters in the figures of animals and trees <note>Ficino is describing hieroglyphics as a sacred symbolic script</note>. This man was held in such great veneration by men that he was recorded among the number of the gods. Many temples were constructed to that divinity. Out of a certain reverence, it was not permitted for the common people to pronounce his proper name rashly or at random. Among the Egyptians, the first month of the year is named after him; a town was founded by him which even now is called in Greek *Hermopolis* <note>meaning 'the City of Mercury'</note>. <margin>Hermopolis</margin>

They called him *Trismegistus* (the Thrice-Greatest) because he stood out as the greatest philosopher, the greatest priest, and the greatest king. For it was the custom of the Egyptians (as Plato writes) to choose priests <margin>Plato.</margin> from the ranks of philosophers, and from the company of priests to choose a king. Therefore, just as he had surpassed all philosophers in sharp wit and learning, so, having been appointed a priest from among them, he excelled all priests in the holiness of his life and his worship of the divine; and finally, having attained royal dignity, he [surpassed] through his administration of laws and his great deeds the glory of previous kings— <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>Marsilio Ficino introduces Hermes Trismegistus as a historical contemporary of Moses and a foundational figure of Egyptian civilization who unified the roles of philosopher, priest, and king. This preface serves to justify the study of these "pagan" texts by linking Hermes to biblical chronology and high moral virtue.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Hermes Trismegistus, Thoth, Hermopolis, Egyptian hierarchy, sacred letters, Cosimo de' Medici, ancient theology</keywords>

<header>MFR. <note>original: "MFR." stands for Marsilius Ficinus Florentinus (Marsilio Ficino the Florentine)</note></header>

[He] obscured [others], so that he was deservedly called "Thrice-Greatest." 
He was the first among philosophers to turn himself from 
natural philosophy <term>Physicis: the study of the physical world and its laws</term> and 
mathematics to the contemplation of divine things. 
He was the first to dispute most wisely concerning the 
majesty of God, the order of demons <note>Ficino uses "demons" in the Neoplatonic sense: spiritual intermediaries between humans and the divine, not necessarily evil spirits</note>, and the 
changes of souls <note>original: "animarum mutationibus," referring to the migration or reincarnation of souls</note>. He was therefore called the 
first author of theology. Following him,

Ancient Theology <term>Prisca Theologia: the Renaissance idea that a single, true theology exists which flows through ancient philosophers to Christianity</term>. 
Aglaophemus was initiated into the sacred rites of Orpheus. 
Pythagoras succeeded Aglaophemus in theology, 
whom Philolaus followed, the teacher of our 
divine Plato. Therefore, a single sect of Ancient Theology, 
consistent with itself in every way, was formed 
from these six theologians in a wonderful order, 
taking its beginnings from Mercury and being 
perfectly completed by the divine Plato. 
Mercury wrote many books 
pertaining to the knowledge of divine things, 
in which—O immortal God—what secret 
mysteries and what stupendous oracles are revealed! 
He speaks not only as a philosopher, 
but oftentimes as a prophet 
and sings of things to come. He foresaw the ruin 
of the old religion, the rise of the new faith, 
the coming of Christ, the future 
judgment, the resurrection of the world, 
the glory of the blessed, and the 
punishments of sinners. Because of this, 
<margin>Augustine</margin>
Aurelius Augustine doubted whether he 
uttered these many things through skill in the stars 
or by the revelation of demons. 
Lactantius, however, does not hesitate 
to number him among the Sibyls and the prophets. 
Finally, out of the many

books of Mercury, two are especially divine. 
One concerns the divine will; 
the other the power and wisdom of God. 
The former is titled *Asclepius*; 
<margin>Apuleius</margin>

<summary>Ficino outlines the "Ancient Theology" (Prisca Theologia) lineage from Hermes to Plato and characterizes Hermes as a pagan prophet who anticipated Christian doctrines. He also notes the recent arrival of the Greek *Pimander* manuscript in Italy via Leonardo of Pistoia.</summary>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s dedication to Cosimo de' Medici. After discussing the ancient lineage of Hermes Trismegistus (Mercury) as the founder of theology, Ficino now explains how the Greek manuscript reached him via a monk and why he is dedicating the translation to Cosimo.</meta>

[The manuscript] <note>Ficino is referring to the manuscript of the *Corpus Hermeticum* brought to Florence by the monk Leonardo da Pistoia</note> of a holy and upright monk reached us. I, however, being spurred on by your encouragements, had decided to translate it from the Greek language into Latin; I thought it would be right, O fortunate Cosimo, to dedicate this little work to your name. For since I myself, aided by your resources and supplied with an abundance of books, have devoted myself to Greek studies, it is fitting for me to offer the first fruits of my Greek studies to that same person. Nor would it be right to dedicate the work of such a wise philosopher, such a pious priest, and such a powerful king to anyone unless he himself (to whom it is dedicated) surpassed others in piety, wisdom, power, and all other things. 

The title of this book, moreover (to come down to the writings of Mercury), is *Pimander* <note>original: "Pimander," now commonly known as *Poimandres* or "The Shepherd of Men"</note>, because out of the four persons who debate in the dialogue, the primary parts are assigned to Pimander. He indeed published the book in Egyptian letters <note>meaning Hieroglyphics or Egyptian script</note>, and one who was also skilled in the Greek language communicated the mysteries of the Egyptians to the Greeks by translating from there. 

¶ The purpose of this work is to discourse upon the power and wisdom of God. And since the operations of these are twofold—the first of which remains in the very nature of God, the second of which reaches out to external things; the former conceives the first and eternal world <term>The Intelligible World: the perfect, spiritual blueprint of reality</term>, while the latter gives birth to the second and temporal world <term>The Sensible World: the physical universe subject to time</term>—he discourses most weightily on both operations and on both worlds. What God's power is, what His wisdom is, in what order they conceive inwardly, and with what progression they give birth outwardly. Furthermore, how the things that have been produced relate to one another, how they agree, or how they differ, and finally in what manner they look back toward their author. 

The order of the volume is this: we distinguish it into fourteen dialogues; the first parts of the dialogue are given to Pimander. The second parts are held by Trismegistus. The third by Asclepius. The fourth place is held by Tatius <note>The characters represent different levels of spiritual instruction: Pimander is the Divine Mind, Hermes is the teacher, and Asclepius and Tat are his disciples</note>. Therefore, Mercury instructs Asclepius in divine matters.

<summary>Marsilio Ficino dedicates his translation of the Hermetic writings to Cosimo de' Medici, explaining the work's origins and its philosophical focus on the dual nature of God's creative power.</summary>
<keywords>Cosimo de' Medici, Marsilio Ficino, Poimandres, Egyptian mysteries, Divine Wisdom, Neoplatonism, Asclepius, Tat, Hermetica</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page concludes Marsilio Ficino’s dedication of the *Pimander* to Cosimo de' Medici. Ficino has just explained the origin of the text and its title; he now moves into the epistemological requirements for understanding divine mysteries—specifically the need for the soul to transcend the senses to become "Mind."</meta>

# MER.

and to instruct Tat <note>Tat was the son and disciple of Hermes Trismegistus in the Hermetic dialogues</note>. One cannot teach divine things if they have not learned them; and by human ingenuity alone, we cannot discover those things which are above nature. Therefore, there is a need for divine light, just as we can only behold the sun itself by the sun’s own light. 

Indeed, the light of the divine mind is never poured into the soul unless that soul (like the moon facing the sun) is turned completely toward the mind of God. The soul is not turned toward the mind unless she herself also becomes "mind" <term>Mens: in the Neoplatonic philosophy of Ficino's circle, this refers to the 'intellect,' the highest part of the soul capable of direct spiritual intuition, distinct from mere reason</term>. But she does not become "mind" until she has cast aside the deceptions of the senses and the mists of the imagination <term>Phantasia: the faculty that creates mental images from sensory input; Ficino argues these images must be cleared to perceive pure spiritual truth</term>. 

For this reason, Mercury <note>Hermes Trismegistus</note> has just now stripped off the darkness of the senses and imagination, withdrawing himself into the inner sanctuary <note>original: "adytum," a Greek-derived word for the most sacred, restricted part of a temple</note> of the mind. Soon, Pimander—that is, the Divine Mind—flows into him. From there, he contemplates the order of all things: both those existing within God and those flowing forth from God. Finally, he explains to other men those things which have been revealed by the divine power. This, then, is the title of the book; this is its purpose and its arrangement. **As for you, fortunate Cosimo: read with joy, and live long, so that our fatherland may also live long.**

<summary>Ficino explains that the Hermetic teachings require a soul to transcend sensory deception to receive divine light. He concludes his dedication by wishing Cosimo de' Medici a long life for the sake of Florence.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Cosimo de' Medici, Pimander, Neoplatonism, divine light, soul, intellect, Florence, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>

The previous page concluded by describing how Hermes Trismegistus prepares his mind to receive divine revelation by withdrawing from the senses. This page opens the first book of the Hermetic corpus.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>I</header>
<sig>I.</sig>

# ¶ The Book of Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurij Trismegisti"</note> on the Power and Wisdom of God: translated from Greek into Latin by Marsilio Ficino the Florentine: to Cosimo de' Medici, Father of the Fatherland, the Pimander

While I was thinking about the nature of things and raising the sharp focus of my mind to the heavens—the senses of my body already having been lulled to sleep, just as usually happens to those who are heavy with sleep due to overeating or exhaustion—suddenly I seemed to see someone of immense bodily size, who, calling me by name, cried out in this way: "What is it, O Hermes <note>original: "Mercuri"; Ficino uses the Latin name Mercury for the Greek Hermes</note>, that you desire to hear and behold? What is it that you wish to learn and understand?" Then I said, "Who are you?" He replied, "I am Pimander <note>Also known as Poimandres, often interpreted as "The Shepherd of Men"</note>, the Mind of divine power; look now to what you wish, for I shall be present with you everywhere." I said, "I desire to learn the nature of things and to know God." To this he said: "Embrace me with your mind, and I will instruct you in all that you have wished for." 

When he had said these things, he changed his form, and suddenly revealed all things. For I beheld a certain immense spectacle—namely, all things converted into light, exceedingly sweet and pleasant, which wonderfully delighted me as I gazed upon it. A little later, a certain dreadful shadow glided down in an oblique revolution, and it changed into a moist nature <term>Humid nature: in Hermetic thought, this represents the primordial, watery chaos from which the material world is formed</term>, then agitated with an unspeakable countenance. From there, a certain great smoke burst forth into a sound; from the sound came a voice, which I judged to be the voice of light; from this voice of light, a Word <note>original: "verbum," corresponding to the Greek "Logos," signifying the divine reason or creative order</note> came forth. Standing by this moist nature, the Word cherished it. Then, from the depths of the moist nature, a pure and light fire immediately flew out and sought the heights.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the Pimander, where Hermes Trismegistus enters a meditative trance and encounters Pimander, the Divine Mind. The vision begins with a revelation of all things as light, which then transitions into the emergence of the material elements and the Divine Word.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Pimander, Marsilio Ficino, Cosimo de' Medici, Divine Mind, cosmogony, the Word, Logos, primordial light, four elements</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the cosmogony or creation myth of the Hermetic tradition, where the divine entity Pimander (the Divine Mind) explains the origins of the universe to Hermes Trismegistus. It follows the initial vision of light and darkness, moving into the separation of elements and the birth of the Demiurge or 'Craftsman' Mind.</meta>

### MERCURY.

Air also, light and yielding to the spirit, took the middle region between fire and water. Truly, earth and water lay so mixed together that the face of the earth, overwhelmed by the waters, was nowhere visible. These two were then set in motion by the spiritual Word <note>original: "spiritali verbo"; this refers to the Logos, the divine reason or speech that brings order to chaos</note> which was carried over them, echoing in their ears. Then Pimander said: "Do you perceive what this vision means?" "I shall know it," I replied.

Pimander then said: "That Light is I, the Mind, your God, older than the moist nature <note>original: "natura humida"; the watery primordial chaos</note> which shone forth from the shadow. The shining seed of the Mind is the Son of God."

"How so?" I asked. He replied: "Think of it this way: that which sees and hears within you is the Word of the Lord <note>original: "verbū domini"</note>, but the Mind is God the Father. For they are not separated from each other, and their union is Life."

**TRISMEGISTUS:** I give thanks to you.

**PIMANDER:** But first, meditate upon the Light and know it.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** When these things were said, I prayed to him for a long time that he would turn his form <note>original: "ideam"; here meaning the divine archetype or visual essence</note> toward me. When he had done so, I suddenly perceived in my mind a Light existing in countless powers, an infinite world of fire <note>original: "ornatum sine termino ignem"; literally an 'unlimited arrangement of fire,' referring to the celestial cosmos</note> surrounded by a mighty force, yet ruling in its own stability. These things I perceived through the word of Pimander, who spoke to me again while I was struck with amazement: "Have you seen in your mind the primary form, which prevails with infinite power?"

"So it seemed to me, Pimander," I replied. Then I asked him: "From where did the elements of nature flow?"

**PIMANDER:** From the will of God, which, having embraced the Word and gazed upon the beautiful world, imitated it, adorning the rest of itself with its own elements and life-giving seeds <note>original: "vitalibusq; feminibus"; the generative principles of life</note>. But the Mind—God, who is most full of the fecundity of both sexes <note>A reference to the androgynous nature of the divine source in Hermetic thought</note>, being Life and Light—gave birth by his Word to another <term>Mind the Craftsman</term> <note>original: "mētem alteram opificem"; the Demiurge who shapes the physical world</note>, who is the god of fire and the power of the spirit. Next, he fashioned seven <term>governors</term> <note>original: "gubernatores"; the planetary spirits who rule over destiny and the visible world</note> who encompass the sensible world <note>The physical world perceived by the senses</note> in circles...

<summary>Pimander explains that God is the Light and the Mind, whose 'Word' is inseparable from Him, and describes the creation of a second 'Craftsman Mind' who fashions the physical world and the seven planetary governors.</summary>
<keywords>Pimander, Hermes Trismegistus, Logos, Creation, Elements, Demiurge, Seven Governors, Hermeticism, Mind of God</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the Hermetic account of creation. On the previous page, Pimander (the Divine Mind) revealed how the Light and the Word (Logos) began to order the primordial chaos. This page describes the emergence of "Fate," the creation of non-rational animals, the birth of the archetypal "Man," and his descent toward the material world.</meta>

# I

...they are connected, and their arrangement is called **Fate** <term>fatum: in this context, the inescapable laws of the physical universe governed by the stars</term>. From there, the Word of God joined with the elements that tend downward—a pure craftsmanship of nature—and it was united with the **Craftsman Mind** <note>original: "opifici menti"; often referred to as the Demiurge, the divine laborer who shapes the world</note>. For they were of the same substance. The elements of nature that fell downward were left without reason, so that they might be as mere matter.

The **Craftsman Mind**, together with the **Word**, containing the circles and turning them with swift speed, turned its machine toward itself and commanded it to roll from beginning to end without end. For it always begins there where it ends. Through the circuit of all these things, just as the Mind itself willed, it forged from the lower elements animals devoid of reason. For it did not grant them reason. The air brought forth flying things, and the water brought forth swimming things. 

Water and earth were also distinguished from each other in the manner that had pleased the Mind. Afterward, the Earth brought forth living creatures (which it had held within): namely four-footed things, serpents, wild beasts, field animals, as well as domestic ones. 

But the Father of all—being **Mind** <note>original: "intellectus"</note>, **Life**, and **Radiance**—procreated a **Man** similar to himself, and rejoiced in him as His own son. For he was beautiful, bearing the image of his Father. God, truly delighted by His own form, granted all His works for human use. 

But Man, when he had considered the creation of all things in his time, also wished to create. Hence, from the contemplation of the Father, he fell down <note>original: "delapsus est"; this suggests the "Fall of Man" in a Hermetic sense—a voluntary descent from the divine realm to the material</note> to the **sphere of generation**. And since he held all power within himself, he observed the handiwork of the **seven governors** <term>gubernatorum: the planetary spirits who rule over the seven celestial spheres</term>. These governors, rejoicing in the contemplation of the human mind, each made the Man a participant in their own order. After he learned their essence and perceived their own nature, he then desired to penetrate and break through the boundary of the circles, and the power of the governor presiding over the fire...

<summary>This page describes the creation of the physical world and its animals by the Craftsman Mind, followed by the birth of the archetypal Man, who descends through the planetary spheres to share in the power of the seven governors.</summary>

<keywords>Fate, Craftsman Mind, Demiurge, Creation of Animals, Archetypal Man, Seven Governors, Sphere of Generation, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the Hermetic cosmogony. On the previous page, the Father (Mind) had created an archetypal "Man" in His own image and granted him authority over all His works. This page describes the Man’s decision to break through the celestial spheres and his subsequent descent into the material world of Nature.</meta>

<header>HERMES</header>

...to grasp. He who had authority and power over all things emerged and broke through the **Harmony** <term>harmonia: here refers to the Seven Spheres of the planets that govern the material world</term> into the mortal and non-rational living things of the world / penetrating and dissolving the power of the circles. And he showed Nature / which slides downward: like the beautiful form of God. When he beheld Nature endowed with wonderful beauty / and possessing all the actions of the **seven governors** <term>septem gubernatorum: the seven planetary deities (Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon) who control Fate</term> / and moreover possessing the very image of God Himself: he smiled upon her with immense love, as one who might see the reflection of human beauty in the water / or perceive a certain likeness of it upon the earth. Moreover, having glimpsed a form similar to himself existing within her—as if in water—he loved it. And he desired to unite with it. The effect followed the will immediately. 

He then procreated a form lacking reason. Nature, having embraced that which she was borne toward with all her love, completely folded and mixed herself into him. For this reason, alone among all earthly living creatures, man is considered to be of a **dual nature**: mortal indeed because of the body / but immortal because of the **substantial man** <note>original: "hominem ipsum substantialem"; the essential, spiritual archetype of humanity as opposed to the physical body</note> himself. For he is immortal / and holds authority over all things: yet the other living things, which are mortal, are subject to **Fate** / and they suffer. Therefore, the superior Harmony did not stand in his way; but having fallen into the Harmony, he was endangered / and became a slave. He, being fortified with the fertility of both sexes / by Him who is the source of trees and rivers / and made watchful by Him who is watchful: is contained and subjected to His dominion.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** After these things, I said: "You yourself are the Mind of my reason." Then **Pimander** said: "This is the mystery which, until this very day, has been hidden from the human race. For Nature, mixing herself with man, brought forth a miracle / which overcomes the wonder of all miracles; for since he himself [belonged] to the harmony of those seven..."

<summary>This page describes the "Fall of Man" in Hermetic thought, where the divine Archetypal Man descends through the planetary spheres, falls in love with Nature's reflection, and becomes a dual being—both divine and mortal. It establishes the Hermetic belief in humanity's innate authority over creation despite being physically bound by Fate.</summary>
<keywords>Pimander, dual nature, Seven Governors, Harmony, Fall of Man, immortality, Fate, Trismegistus, Nature, androgyny</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and the divine Mind, Pimander. The previous page established the archetypal Man's descent into Nature. This page describes the creation of the first seven androgynous humans and the transition of the world from a unified state to one of sexual differentiation and mortal generation.</meta>

# I

...had been imbued by him whom I told you of a little while ago—namely, the Father and the Spirit—Nature herself did not resist. Rather, she immediately brought forth seven men: sharing the natures of the **seven governors** <term>septem gubernatorum: the planetary deities who rule over the material world</term>, both masculine and feminine in gender <note>original: "masculini pariter ac feminini"; referring to the original androgynous state of humanity</note> and sublime. To this I myself replied: "O Pimander, I have just been struck by a burning desire; I long to hear the rest besides. Therefore, do not desert me here, I beseech you." But he said to me:

**PIMANDER:** Be silent, for I have not yet finished the first discourse.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Behold, I am now silent.

**PIMANDER:** The generation of these seven (as I said) was brought about in this manner. For the female [element] was air and water was the means of union; from fire she took maturity, and from ether she received spirit, and Nature bound the bodies together to fashion them into the appearance of man. Not however from life and light did it proceed into soul and mind <note>original: "mortēq3" (death); this is likely a scribal or printing error for "mentem" (mind), as Hermetic doctrine holds that Life becomes Soul and Light becomes Mind</note>. Indeed, life bestowed the soul; light, finally, the mind. In this way all the members of the world remained through its senses until the end of the cycle of beginnings and kinds.

But hear now the rest of the discourse which you so greatly desired. When the cycle was finally completed, the bond of all things was loosened by the will of God. For all living creatures, which had been of both genders, were dissolved along with man. They were then made masculine in part, and likewise feminine. Immediately God cried out with a holy word: "Increase, multiply, and propagate, all you offspring and my works! And you moreover, to whom a portion of **mind** <term>mentis: the divine intellect or 'Nous' that distinguishes humans from animals</term> has been granted: recognize your race and consider your immortal nature. Know that love of the body is the cause of death. Learn the nature of all things."

With these things said, **Providence** <term>providentia: the divine foresight that orders the universe</term>, through **Fate** and **Harmony**, forged the mixtures and established the generations. From which all things were...

<summary>Pimander explains how Nature birthed seven androgynous humans from the elements and how God eventually split these beings into two sexes, commanding them to multiply while warning that attachment to the body leads to death.</summary>
<keywords>androgyny, seven governors, elements, sexual differentiation, immortality, providence, fate, divine command</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus (referred to here as Mercurius) and Pimander, the Divine Mind. The previous page ended with the conclusion of the first cycle of creation and the start of the differentiation of species and the onset of human generation.</meta>

# I

...propagated according to their own kind. Finally, he who has known himself has attained that Good which is **above all essence** <term>super essentiam: a state of being beyond physical or even metaphysical categories, often associated with the One or the Absolute in Neoplatonic thought</term>. But he who, through the error of love, embraced the body: he wandered in darkness, perceiving the evils of death through the senses.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** How many fail, nonetheless—being ignorant, I say—so that for this reason they are deprived of immortality?

**PIMANDER:** You seem, O Mercury, not to fully understand what you have heard.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Even if I have not yet professed to understand, I do nonetheless understand and remember.

**PIMANDER:** I am glad if you hold fast to what has been said.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Answer me, I pray, Pimander: why are they worthy of death, those who lie in death?

**PIMANDER:** Because a dismal shadow preceded his own body; from this shadow comes the **moist nature** <note>original: "natura humida"; in Hermetic physics, this refers to the primordial watery chaos or the dense material substance from which the physical world was formed</note>. From this, the body was established in the sensible world; from this, finally, death itself flowed forth. Do you hold this, Mercury? Do you also hold the reason why he who knows himself passes into God, as the **Word of God** <note>original: "dei verbum"; likely referring to the Logos or the creative command mentioned earlier in the text</note> has handed down?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Because the Father of all, from whom man was born, consists of Life and Light.

**PIMANDER:** You speak correctly. God and Father is Light and Life, from whom man was born. If therefore you understand yourself to be composed of Life and Light, you will pass back again into Life and Light.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** These things Pimander said. But I said: I pray you, O Mind, still tell me: in what way may I ascend to Life?

**PIMANDER:** <margin>The divine Mind assisting the pious.</margin> God the Mind himself commanded that man, as a participant in the Mind, should **know himself** <note>original: "seipsum animadverteret"; this echoes the famous Delphic maxim "Know Thyself" as the necessary first step for the soul to begin its return to the divine</note>.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Do not all individual men therefore possess Mind?

**PIMANDER:** You speak correctly, Mercury. For I, the Mind, am present with those who are good, pious, pure, religious, and holy; and my presence brings them help, so that they immediately discern all things and find the Father peaceful and favorable. Therefore, they give thanks, blessing him piously, and praising him with solemn hymns; and they indeed surrender the body to its own death, sen...

<meta>catchword: sen (likely 'sentientes' - feeling/perceiving)</meta>

<summary>This page explores the Hermetic requirement of self-knowledge for spiritual salvation, explaining that while the body is born of shadow and moist nature (leading to death), the true self is composed of Life and Light. Pimander explains that the Divine Mind assists only the pious and holy in their ascent back to the Father.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Pimander (the Divine Mind) to Hermes Trismegistus regarding the fate of the soul and the nature of the "avenging demon" that punishes the wicked. It then transitions into the famous Hermetic description of the soul's ascent through the seven planetary spheres.</meta>

# I
### Page 7

...they disdain its allurements, as they clearly judge how lethal the charms of the senses are. I, the Mind, acting as the gatekeeper, do not permit the snares of the body to achieve their end. The entrances through which foul flatteries usually flow, I continually block off, and I extinguish all the fuel of lust.

<margin>The avenging demon standing by the wicked</margin>
On the contrary, I stay far away from the ignorant, the wicked, the lazy, the envious, the unjust, the murderers, and the impious. I leave them to the judgment of the **avenging demon** <note>original: "demon vltor"; in Hermeticism, this is not a fallen angel in the Christian sense, but a *daimon*—a spiritual executioner or a personification of the soul's own conscience and consequences</note>, who, striking with the sharp edge of fire, afflicts the senses and arms the person for committing further crimes, so that as a culprit of a more shameful fault, they may be liable to a sharper punishment. And without any intermission, he inflames them toward insatiable desires. He fights against them in the darkness; he examines the sin, and in a wondrous manner, he stirs up and increases the force of fire for their torment.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** You have explained everything to me carefully, O Mind, just as I asked. Answer me further, then: what will happen after the ascent?

<margin>The Ascent: by letting go of all earthly things.</margin>
**PIMANDER:** First indeed, in the dissolution of the material body, the body falls into alteration. The form which it previously had disappears from sight and is hidden for the future. The idle disposition of character is yielded and given up to the demon. The bodily senses, having become parts of the soul, flow back to their sources, to rise again one day into their own activities; the powers of anger and desire return to the nature that lacks reason. Thus, what remains then returns through the **harmony** <term>harmonia: the structured arrangement of the seven celestial spheres or planetary circles that the soul must pass through</term> to the higher realms. To the first zone, the soul gives back the power of growing and shrinking. To the second, the plotting of evils and idle deceit. To the third, the idle deception of lust. To the fourth, domineering and insatiable ambition. To the fifth, profane arrogance and daring...

<summary>Pimander explains how the Divine Mind protects the virtuous while an avenging demon punishes the wicked. The text begins to describe the soul's ascent through the seven planetary spheres, where it sheds various worldly vices.</summary>
<keywords>Pimander, Hermes Trismegistus, Soul's Ascent, Avenging Demon, Seven Spheres, Harmonia, Hermetica, Neoplatonism, Purification</keywords>

# I
### Page 8

...[from the fifth zone] rashness; in the **sixth zone**, wicked and idle opportunities for wealth; and in the **seventh zone**, inherent falsehood.

Then, indeed, the soul, stripped of the motions of the harmony <note>original: "harmonie motiones"; referring to the governing influences of the seven planetary spheres</note>, returns to its desired nature, possessing its own power. Together with those who are already there, it praises the Father. They also add themselves to the number of the **Powers** <note>original: "potestatum"; these are the divine emanations or angelic ranks surrounding God</note>; and having become Powers, they enjoy God. And this is the supreme good for those who possess the lot of knowledge: namely, to become God.

What else remains for you, except that, having embraced all things, you should wish to be a guide to those who are worthy of your care, so that through your gift, the human race might attain divine salvation?

Having spoken these things, **Pimander** <note>The "Divine Mind" or "Shepherd of Men"</note> withdrew into the number of the divine Powers. But I, blessing and giving thanks to the Parent of all, rose up, already strengthened by him and instructed in the order of all nature, having contemplated a most clear vision.

<margin>The Exhortation of Hermes</margin>
From that point, I began to announce to men the beauty of piety and knowledge. "O peoples, men of the earth, you who have given yourselves over to drunkenness, sleep, and ignorance: live soberly! Abstain from the luxury of the belly, you who are soothed by irrational sleep."

But they, listening, gathered together with me in one mind. Then I added again: "Why, O men of the earth, do you rush headlong into death, when you have the capacity to attain immortality? Call yourselves back, you who labor in poverty and are enveloped in the darkness of ignorance. Depart from the dark light; take hold of immortality, and flee corruption."

Some of them, indeed, mocking, went away, having been cast headlong onto the path of death. Others, however, prostrating themselves at my feet, begged that I might instruct them. Raising them up, therefore, I became the guide of the human race. Indeed, I showed them what was the way of attaining salvation, and I poured words of wisdom into their ears. By which it happened, that they...

### Page 8

...having emerged from the storms of rain <note>original: "ex imbrium procellis"; a metaphor for the turbulent and "heavy" material world</note>. At last, as evening approached and the rays of the sun were setting, I commanded them to give thanks to God. After they had given thanks, each one retired to his own bed. 

But I inscribed the kindness of **Pimander** <note>The "Shepherd of Men" or Divine Mind</note> into the innermost recesses of my soul; and having attained everything I sought, I rested in great joy. For the sleep of my body—since it occurred in a state of sobriety—was the true insight of my eyes; my silence was a fertile pregnancy of goodness; and the utterance of my speech was the birth of all good things. 

These things happened to me because I drew them from the **Mind** <note>original: "ex mente"; referring to the universal intellect</note>—that is, from Pimander, the Word <note>original: "verbo"</note> of divine power. By this means, I was inspired by the divine spirit and became a possessor of the truth. For this reason, with all the powers of my soul, I give thanks to God the Father.

<margin>The Thanksgiving of Mercury</margin>
Holy is God, the Father of all. 
Holy is God, whose will is fulfilled by His own powers. 
Holy is God, who makes Himself known to His own people. 
Holy art Thou, who established all things by the **Word**. 
Holy art Thou, of whom all nature is an image. 
Holy art Thou, whom nature did not create. 
Holy art Thou, stronger than all power. 
Holy art Thou, greater than all excellence. 
Holy art Thou, better than all praise. 

Accept these holy sacrifices of words, flowing from a soul and heart that is bound to Thee. O Ineffable One, who can be praised by silence alone by the one who has turned away from the deceits contrary to true knowledge <note>original: "cognitioni"; often used to translate the Greek "Gnosis"</note>. 

Grant my prayer: strengthen me, and make those who dwell in ignorance partakers of this grace—those who are my brothers by kinship, but Thy sons by nature. Indeed, I offer my faith to Thee. I bear witness of Thee. I rise into life and light. Thou Thyself art the venerable Father. Thy "human" <note>Hermes refers to himself/humanity as "homo tuus"</note> desires to share in Thy holiness, since Thou hast granted him all power and authority.

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page serves as a thematic bridge and introductory summary, comparing the revelations of Hermes Trismegistus (Mercury) in the "Pimander" with the biblical accounts of Moses in Genesis. It establishes a harmony between Egyptian "Hermetic" wisdom and Judeo-Christian scripture.</meta>

**MERCURY**

<margin>I</margin>
In this first of fourteen dialogues (while always preserving the piety of religion <note>original: "salua semper religiōis pietate"; a disclaimer often used by Renaissance scholars to show that studying Hermes does not conflict with Christian faith</note>), it appears that **Mercury** <note>The Roman name for Hermes Trismegistus</note> is treating the Mosaic mysteries. Indeed, **Moses** saw darkness upon the face of the abyss and the spirit of the Lord carried over the waters; here, however, Mercury sees a "horrendous shadow" transforming into a "moist nature," which this same moist nature the Word of the Lord cherished. Moses announces all things created by the powerful Word of the Lord; here, Mercury asserts that the shining Word (which illuminates all things) is the "offspring of the Mind" and the "Son of God"—and that the Mind as Father and the Word of the Mind as Son do not differ in nature. He says it was **consubstantial** <note>original: "consubstantiale"; a key theological term meaning "of the same substance," usually used to describe the relationship between Christ and God the Father</note>. He also saw the union of these two. And if you desire more fully—and if it is permitted to ascribe such great divine knowledge to men who lived before the Word was veiled in flesh <note>The Incarnation of Christ</note>—he saw the Son being born of the Father, and the Spirit proceeding from the Father and the Son in a wondrous manner, when he said: "The Mind, however, being God, most full of the fecundity of both sexes, being Life and Light, brought forth with His own Word a second Mind, the **Artificer** <note>original: "opificem"; the Demiurge or creator-craftsman who shapes the material world</note>." He also saw the Mind reaching out to external things through its exuberant fecundity, and forming all things according to the pattern of His own divine Word, distributing to each—in the air, in the water, and in the earth—its own nature according to its kind. Soon after, he saw **Man** made in His own likeness and image, to whose use the Artificer Mind subjected all sensible things. This Man soon fell from the intelligible sphere <note>The realm of pure thought and spirit</note> to the sphere of corporeal and lustful things. From this, he contracted the origin of the "kindling of evils"; and at last, Man (by God's will) was released from all knots, though he became subject to death along with all other created things. What else is it for God to have cried out with a holy Word, "Sprout, grow, and propagate all seeds and my works," than that Mosaic command, "Increase and multiply and fill the earth"? Next, Mercury instructs us on how the path to the **Mind** lies open, what evil error finally leads us away from it, to whom the Mind (the generous giver of all good things) is abundantly present, and from whom it is likewise absent. He explains how, just as we fall and degenerate from our intellectual and immortal nature into fleeting things through certain steps, so too are we restored to the former state of a purified mind through certain opposite steps. Moses became the leader of the Hebrew flock by divine decree; Mercury became the leader of the Egyptians. And now he feeds his flock with holy instruction, living an active life; now truly, praising the Father of all things with hymns and thanksgivings, he rises into the life and light of contemplation. This is the summary of the **Pimander** <note>The first book of the Hermetic corpus, titled "The Shepherd of Men"</note>.

# ¶ Mercury's Universal Discourse to Aesculapius. II

<meta>Early European Books, Copyright © 2011 ProQuest LLC. Images reproduced by courtesy of the Biblioteca Nazionale Centrale di Firenze. CFMAGL. 1.6.404</meta>

<summary>This page provides a philosophical commentary comparing the Hermetic creation story in the Pimander to the Book of Genesis, arguing for a shared divine truth between Moses and Hermes Trismegistus. It outlines the descent of Man into materiality and the path toward spiritual restoration.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Moses, Pimander, Creation, Consubstantial, Artificer, Fall of Man, Contemplation, Aesculapius</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the nature of the Divine and the mechanics of the cosmos. It transitions from the theological descriptions of the Mind and the Word to a more technical discussion on the relationship between God, essence, and the movement of the celestial spheres.</meta>

# I

<margin>d</margin>
Divinity itself is **Being** <note>original: "Ens"; in this context, it refers to the ultimate state of existence or "is-ness"</note>; I speak now not of that which is begotten, but clearly of that which is **unbegotten**. If, therefore, it is divine, it is essence. If it is God, it is even beyond essence. It is "intelligible" <note>original: "intelligibile"; meaning that which can be grasped by the intellect rather than the physical senses</note> in this manner: for the first God is intelligible not to Himself, but to us. Indeed, that which is intelligible falls under the understanding of the one who perceives through the senses. Therefore, God is not at all intelligible to Himself, for He is not something existing as separate from that which He understands, nor is He understood by Himself as if He were an object. Yet He is something different from us; for this reason, He is understood by us. 

If the **Word** <note>original: "logos"; refers here to the divine Reason or the second person of the Hermetic trinity</note> is intelligible, it is not God. If it is as God, it is certainly not called *logos* or the Word as if it were a separate thing. Furthermore, everything that moves is moved not in a moved thing, but in something stable. That which moves the object also remains permanent, for it is impossible for the mover to be agitated at the same time as the thing it moves.

**ESCULAPIUS:** In what way then, **Trismegistus**, are those things in the world changed together with those that move? For you said the wandering spheres <note>The planets, which appear to "wander" across the sky</note> are moved by the **fixed sphere** <note>original: "aplane sphera"; the outermost shell of the heavens containing the fixed stars</note>.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** That is not motion, O **Esculapius**, but resistance. For they do not travel in the same direction, but in a contrary manner. This opposition contains a stable reverberation of motion. For the percussion of a stationary point is agitation. Therefore, because the wandering spheres run their courses in a contrary and subordinate way to the fixed sphere, they encounter one another in a contrary meeting; thus they are moved by that which stands firm, stabilized by this very opposition. 

This cannot be otherwise. For the **Bears** <note>original: "arctos"; the constellations Ursa Major and Ursa Minor, which in the northern hemisphere never set below the horizon</note> themselves (which you know neither rise nor sink but perpetually circle the same point)—do you think they move, or rather that they stay still?

**ESCULAPIUS:** They move, Trismegistus.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** With what motion, Esculapius?

**ESCULAPIUS:** With a motion forever running around the same point.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** But truly, that circulation—being the same and around the same point—is a motion held within a state of rest. For the center point prohibits the thing from moving beyond its orbit. That which is hindered in its path stays around the same point, and thus contrary agitation is made firm, perpetually stabilized by the opposition itself.

->b i<-

<summary>This page explores the distinction between God as "unbegotten Being" and the "intelligible" Word, followed by a dialogue on celestial mechanics. Hermes explains that the motion of the planets is stabilized by the resistance of the fixed stars, using the non-setting constellations of the Bears as an example of motion that exists within a state of rest.</summary>

<keywords>essence, unbegotten, logos, celestial spheres, fixed stars, planetary motion, Ursa Major, Esculapius, stability</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Trismegistus and Esculapius regarding the nature of motion and stability. Following the previous discussion on the fixed and wandering spheres, Hermes now moves to terrestrial examples to explain how motion requires a stable foundation.</meta>

[[page number: 10]]

### MERCURY

I will set examples before your eyes from the world of the living: just as when a man swims (while the water is running swiftly, of course), the striking of his hands and feet together causes him to stay upright, lest he collapse along with the water or be submerged within it. **ESCULAPIUS:** You have brought a very clear example into our midst, O Trismegistus. **TRISMEGISTUS:** Therefore, whatever is moved is moved within a stable state, and by something stable. Thus, every movement of a living thing (which consists of matter) is by no means caused by those things which are outside the world, but rather from internal things to external—whether by the **soul** <note>original: "anima"; the life-force or animating principle</note>, or by the spirit, or by some incorporeal mind. For a body does not move an animated body in the least; not even if it were a whole body at once, even if it were to exist as something inanimate. **ESCULAPIUS:** By what reasoning do you assert this, O Trismegistus? **TRISMEGISTUS:** Wood and stones, and all other things which lack a soul <note>The text here says "quēcūq; aīam habēt" (whoever have a soul), but the context of "wood and stones" suggests Hermes is contrasting inanimate objects with the animating principle he just defined.</note>,

<margin>He defines the soul by its motion.</margin>
are not moved by their own bodies, O Esculapius. For what is there inside the body that moves the inanimate thing itself? It is certainly not the body itself by which both are moved—both the body of that which carries and that of which is carried. Nor is that which was mentioned previously animated for the primary reason that it moves. Do you not see then that the soul is most burdened when it alone sustains two bodies? For it is manifest that whatever is moved, is moved in something stable and by something stable. **ESCULAPIUS:** In that thing certainly change must occur for those things that are changed, O Trismegistus. **TRISMEGISTUS:** You speak rightly, O Esculapius. There is nothing in the order of things that is a **vacuum** <term>Vacuum: A void or space containing absolutely nothing; a concept Hermes argues is physically impossible.</term>. For only that which is not—that which is deprived of existence—is to be called a vacuum. Indeed, nothing can be found which, while it exists, is a vacuum. **ESCULAPIUS:** Are there not certain things found to be empty, Trismegistus? Such as an empty jar, an empty well, and many other such things? **TRISMEGISTUS:** Alas, how far you wander from the truth, O Esculapius. Those things which are ample and most full of all things, you consider empty. **ESCULAPIUS:** How is that, O Trismegistus? **TRISMEGISTUS:** **Air** is indeed a body. This body penetrates all other things and, flowing through them, fills all things. For a body is of this kind: made of corporeal elements...

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus explains that all motion requires a stable foundation and originates from an internal, incorporeal force like the soul. He further argues against the existence of a vacuum, asserting that even "empty" vessels are actually filled with the body of air.</summary>
<keywords>motion, stability, soul, vacuum, air, Esculapius, Trismegistus, physics, animating principle</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument from the previous page that there is no such thing as a "void" or "empty" space. Hermes continues explaining to Esculapius that space is always filled with either air or spirit, moving toward a definition of the incorporeal Mind and the nature of God as the ultimate Good.</meta>

[[page number: 10]]

# I

...not composite. From which it follows: that whatever things you call empty are, in fact, full of air. Therefore, those things ought to be called hollow rather than empty; for they both exist as things and are full of air and spirit.

**ESCULAPIUS:** This reasoning is invincible and beyond doubt, O Trismegistus. Air is a body. This body penetrates into all remaining things, and flowing into them, it fills everything. <margin>Place</margin> What shall we say "Place" is, in which everything is moved?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Something incorporeal, O Esculapius. **ESCULAPIUS:** And what is the incorporeal? **TRISMEGISTUS:** It is Mind and Reason embracing themselves, free from all bodily mass, foreign to error, impassible <note>original: "impassibilis"; a philosophical term meaning unable to be affected or changed by external forces</note>, intangible, self-subsisting, purifying and preserving all things. Its rays are the Good, Truth, the primary light, and the first form of souls.

<margin>God</margin> **ESCULAPIUS:** What is God? **TRISMEGISTUS:** God is that which is none of these things, yet is the cause that all these things exist; He is a certain presence to all, and present also to each individual thing, nor does He permit anything not to exist. All things are procreated from those things which exist. From nothing, however, nothing comes; for those things which are least <note>Hermes refers here to things that lack substance or true existence</note> have no nature by which they might be able to come into being. On the contrary, those things which exist have no nature through which they might at some time cease to exist.

**ESCULAPIUS:** What then? Do you say that God is "something"? **TRISMEGISTUS:** God is certainly not Mind, but He is the cause that Mind exists; nor is He Spirit, but He is the cause that Spirit stands forth; nor is He Light, but He is the cause by which Light exists. <margin>Two names of God: the Good and the Father.</margin> Hence, one must worship God by these two names, which belong to Him alone and fit no other being entirely. For even among those who, besides Him, are called "gods"—whether they be demons or men—none can be as good as the one God. For He is the Good itself, nor is He anything other than the Good. All other things are separated from the very nature of the Good. Indeed, body and soul have no place in which they might contain the Good. For that Goodness is as vast as the existence of all things—both corporeal and incorporeal, both sensible <note>things that can be perceived by the senses</note> and intelligible <note>things that can only be understood by the mind</note>. This is the Good; this is God. Take care, therefore, that you never call anything else "good," for this would be a profane error. Nor should you call God anything else but "the Good alone," for you would surely fall into the same impiety.

b ij

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus explains that space is never truly empty but filled with air and spirit, and defines God not as Mind or Light itself, but as the causal "Good" that allows all such things to exist.</summary>
<keywords>void, air, incorporeal, Mind, God, the Good, cause of existence, Hermeticism, Esculapius</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius) and Esculapius. Having established that there is no void in the universe, Hermes now turns to the definition of God as the "ultimate Good" and the "Father," linking the act of human procreation to the divine nature of generation.</meta>

[[page number: 11]]
[[header: MER. Folio]]

Therefore, the **Good** is proclaimed by all in common speech, yet what it actually is, is not understood by all. For this reason, God is not truly known by everyone. But on account of ignorance, they name both certain gods and even some men "good," though they can never truly be so, nor even become so. Therefore, all the remaining immortal gods are honored with the name of "God," but God is the <term>Good</term> <note>original: "bonum"; in Hermetic philosophy, the "Good" is a technical term for the source of all existence which lacks nothing and gives everything</note> not by way of a title or honor, but by His very nature. For the nature of God is one: namely, the Good itself. Indeed, there is one thing present in both names <note>the names "God" and "The Good"</note>, from which all kinds of things flow forth. For the "good" bestows all things and receives nothing; God provides all things and takes nothing. Therefore, God is the Good, and the Good is God.

Another name for God is the **Father**, for this reason: because He begets all things. For it is the duty of a father to generate. 
<margin>The Father</margin>
Wherefore, the most distinguished pursuit in life—and even among the wise—is considered to be the **procreation of children**. Indeed, the greatest calamity and impiety befalls that man who departs from this life without children. For this reason, he is given over to <term>demons</term> <note>original: "demonibus"; here referring to intermediary spirits or guardians of the cosmic order who administer justice after death</note> to suffer punishments after death. Finally, the punishment is of this kind: that the **sterile soul** is judged to migrate into a certain body that possesses neither the nature of a male nor a female. This soul is indeed elevated by the sun and bound to it. Therefore, O Esculapius, have no commerce with a man who has fathered no children; nevertheless, pity his unhappiness, knowing that these evils await him after his passing. Let so many and such things be said to you, O Esculapius, from which a certain foreknowledge of the nature of all things may be gathered.

---

<gloss>f</gloss>
The second part discusses that God is intelligible to man alone through a rational method. From the center, the pole, place, and the forms of things, the author hunts for God Himself (around whom, in whom, and from whom all things move), who is stable, immobile, and always remains ever the same to Himself; he also argues that nothing in the world can be a vacuum. Rather, those things which appear to be empty, if viewed according to the correct fullness, emulate the highest fullness. Soon, he suggests to Esculapius in his responses what God is *not* rather than what He *is*. He explains, however, that God is the absolute Good and the Father, whose duty is to beget all things. This, when understood as an internal generation remaining within the nature of God, brings about the piety of divine religion. Finally, it is argued that the name of "the Good" belongs to God alone.

<summary>Hermes defines God as the "Good" and the "Father," arguing that true goodness is a divine nature rather than a human title. He stresses the religious duty of procreation, claiming that those who die childless face a specific karmic punishment of spiritual sterility.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Esculapius, The Good, The Father, procreation, sterile soul, afterlife punishment, divine nature, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page concludes the previous discussion on procreation between Hermes and Asculapius, transitioning from a literal to a spiritual interpretation of "offspring." It then begins the "Sacred Discourse," a Hermetic creation myth that parallels the Book of Genesis but through the lens of ancient Egyptian-Greek natural philosophy.</meta>

[[page number: 12]]
[[header: I ii]]

strives; this is in harmony with the **truth of the Gospel** <note>original: "evangelice veritati"; the translator is drawing a parallel between Hermetic philosophy and the Christian Gospels</note>. "Why do you call me good? No one is good except God alone." <note>A direct quote from Luke 18:19</note> And what he adds concerning the extreme calamity of those who depart this life without children is a **veiled speech**. For you should not conceive of this as a bodily birth, but as a birth of the **soul**; consider its children and sons to be the offspring of **virtues**, and consider "masculine wisdom" as that which is used for contemplating God and the most divine powers of God. This is what he openly hints at when he says: "the punishment, finally, is of this kind: that the sterile soul, through judgment, migrates into a certain body in which the nature of neither male nor female is present." It is as if he were openly saying: a body through which the soul would never perform good deeds, nor would it ever receive anything of the **Good**. Therefore, flee the sterility of the soul with all your might, and beget offspring (which may one day make you blessed); and in this way, imitate the fecundity of the **Father**, who is always begetting, with the fecundity of your own mind (as far as you are able).

### ¶ This is the summary of Esculapius.

### ¶ The Sacred Discourse of Hermes. III.

The glory of all things is **God**. The divine is divine nature. The beginning of the universe is God: mind, nature, act, necessity, the end, and renewal. For there was an infinite shadow in the abyss; moreover, water and a thin, **intellectual spirit** <note>original: "spiritus tenuis intellectualis"; in this context, 'spirit' refers to an active, subtle substance that permeates matter, guided by reason</note> were present in the chaos through divine power. Then a **holy splendor** shone forth, which brought out the elements from beneath the sand and the moist nature; and all the gods <note>referring to the celestial bodies or spirits</note> were distinguishing the seminal nature. When all things had been indistinct, the light elements afterward flew up into the high region, while the heavy things settled under the moist sand. When things were distinguished and balanced—those which were carried by a fiery spirit—the heaven flashed forth into **seven circles** <note>the seven planetary spheres of the ancient cosmos: Moon, Mercury, Venus, Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>. The gods appeared in the forms of the stars, visible with their signs. The stars were numbered according to the gods who inhabit them. The wider circuit was carried by its own course in an aerial circle, with the divine spirit breathing as one. Each of the gods, from his internal power, fulfilled the work assigned to him. Therefore, living creatures were born: four-footed beasts, reptiles, aquatic creatures, and likewise winged ones. Likewise, every sowing sprouted through seeds: grass, herb, and the shoots of flowers, each containing within itself the seed of **regeneration**. The generation of men was for the knowledge of divine works and for the testimony of nature, to command all things

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on the spiritual necessity of "begetting" virtues and begins a Hermetic cosmogony describing the separation of elements and the creation of the planetary spheres and life.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual procreation, virtue, sterility of soul, cosmogony, chaos, elements, seven circles, planetary gods, regeneration, Hermes Trismegistus</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Sacred Discourse" of Hermes Trismegistus, describing the purpose of the human soul and the cycle of nature. It then transitions into a critical commentary that contrasts these "gentile" or pagan views of divinity and magic with Christian monotheism. The text connects the previous page's description of the abyss to the ordering of the physical world.</meta>

[[header: HERMES:]]

...which are covered by the sky, for the discernment of good things, for the increase of the race, and for the propagation of number. And every **soul** veiled in the shadow of the flesh was granted a certain portion to look up at the course of the celestial gods, at the works of **God**, and the progressions of nature; to look for the signs of good things, and for the knowledge of divine power. This portion was given for the judgment of good and evil, and for the discovery of various crafts of good things. It begins, however, to live in them and to obtain **wisdom**, according to the portion of the continuous course of the circling gods. 

Furthermore, it shall be dissolved into that in which ample monuments and reminders of crafts shall stand forth upon the earth for these chosen ones through the renewal of times. Herbs and the generations of animate flesh—the seeds of fruits and the works of nature and art having decayed—shall grow young again. Finally, by necessity and the renewal of the gods, and by the circular course of numerous divine nature, and surely the very **universal harmony** <note>original: "conspiratio mundi"; the Stoic and Hermetic idea that all parts of the universe "breathe together" in a single living system</note> of the whole world, nature shall bloom anew. For indeed, within **divinity** itself, nature also resides.

The third discourse recounts again: that from the womb of unformed matter, illuminated by forms as if by holy splendors, all things proceeded to their birth in their own order, and that the blessed minds of the heavens fulfill the work committed to them by the highest **Father**. 

And that **man** was born for a twofold duty: namely, for the contemplation of eternal things, and for the judgment of good and evil, and for the moderation of human affairs through both the various inventions of crafts and through **prudence**. To him, indeed, the sky, the earth, herbs, sprouts, the entire harmony of the world, and the most beautiful order of things suggest most abundantly the material for contemplation and for raising oneself toward divine things. For nature is like a **living book** <note>original: "liberynus," likely a contraction or error for "liber vivus" (living book), a common trope where nature is seen as a text written by God</note> full of divinity and a mirror of divine things. 

These words clearly set forth the paganism of the gentiles and create a certain foundation for their errors, tending **toward almost every vanity** <note>original Greek: "πρὸς πάντα σχεδὸν τὴν ματαιότητα"</note>. They lead toward every form of divination, toward **idolatry**, the rites and initiations <note>original: "teletas"; refers to secret mystery religions</note> of the gods, or more truly **will-worship** <note>original: "thelothreskeias"; a term from Colossians 2:23 referring to self-imposed or man-made religious practices</note>, magic, **theurgy** <note>original: "theurgias"; the practice of rituals intended to compel or invite divine action</note>, fate, "craftsman gods," and many such impure and nefarious things. For us, however, there is **one God**, who made heaven and earth and all things that are in them: and who, as He wills, changes and transfers all things into their proper seasons.

<summary>This page concludes the Hermetic vision of a self-renewing nature where souls study the stars and crafts, then shifts to a Christian critique labeling these practices as "vanity" and "idolatry."</summary>
<keywords>Hermeticism, nature as a mirror, divine renewal, theurgy, idolatry, universal harmony, spiritual duty, monotheism</keywords>
