<meta>This page continues the Hermetic teachings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius). While the previous page discussed the soul's role in the physical world and the cycle of nature, this text shifts to a specific dialogue between Hermes and his son/disciple, Tat. It introduces the "Crater" or "Mixing-Bowl" as a central metaphor for divine intellect (Mind) and the human struggle to achieve spiritual perfection through knowledge.</meta>

[[page number: iz IIII]]
[[header: I]]

### ¶ Hermes to Tat: The Mixing-Bowl, or the Monad.

<margin>5</margin>
The Creator <term>Opifex: literally "the worker" or "artisan"; in Hermetic texts, this refers to the Demiurge or Creator God who fashions the universe</term> fashioned the whole world by the Word, not with hands. Think of Him in this way, as one always present and acting in all things: the one God establishing all things by His own will. For this is His body: not tangible, not visible, not measurable, not separated by distance, nor like any other thing. For He is neither fire, nor water, nor air, nor even spirit; rather, all these things depend upon Him. The Good is of such a nature that it belongs to Him alone. He also willed to adorn the earth with the beauty of His divine workmanship. Therefore, He sent down Man—a mortal creature made from a mortal soul <note>original: "mortale animal... mortalis animantis"; this highlights the dual nature of humanity in Hermetic thought—both divine in origin and earthly in body</note>. And the world is indeed filled with living creatures through the intellect and reason of the world. For man was made to be the contemplator of God's work; and while he admired it, he recognized its Author. 

Indeed, O Tat, God distributed Speech to all men, but Mind <term>Mens: corresponding to the Greek "Nous," representing the higher divine intellect or spiritual consciousness rather than mere logic</term> He did not. Not that He was envious, for envy surely does not come from Him, but rather it dwells with the souls of men who lack Mind. 

**TAT.** Why, O father, did God not communicate Mind to all? 

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Because He willed, O son, to set it in the midst as a prize and a reward for souls to strive for. 

**TAT.** Where then did He place it? 

**TRISMEGISTUS.** When He had filled a large mixing-bowl <term>Crater: an ancient vessel for mixing wine and water, here used metaphorically as a vessel of divine wisdom</term> with it, He sent a herald, commanding him to proclaim these things to the souls of men: "Immerse yourself in this bowl, you who are able; you who believe that your soul shall return to Him who sent it down; you who know for what purpose you were born." Therefore, whoever listened to the herald and dipped themselves into the Mind became partakers of knowledge <note>original: "cognitionis"; refers to Gnosis, the direct, experiential knowledge of the divine</note>; and receiving the Mind, they emerged as perfect men.

<summary>A foundational Hermetic dialogue where Hermes Trismegistus explains that while all humans have speech, only those who "immerse themselves" in the divine Mixing-Bowl of Mind (Nous) achieve true knowledge and spiritual perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, The Crater, Mind (Nous), The Word (Logos), Creator (Opifex), Gnosis, Human Nature, Monad</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius) and his son Tat regarding the "Mixing-Bowl" (Crater) of Mind. Having previously explained that God sent down the "Crater" as a prize for souls to seek, Hermes now describes the difference between those who ignore this call and those who embrace the divine intellect.</meta>

[[header: MERCURIUS]]

### ¶ The Proclamation of Mind

But they neglected the proclamation; though they share in speech, they are devoid of Mind <term>Mens: corresponding to the Greek "Nous," this refers to divine intellect or spiritual consciousness</term>, and they remain ignorant of the purpose for which they were created, and by whom. Furthermore, their senses are like those of irrational animals; thus, entangled in desire, they do not admire those things worthy of contemplation. For, enslaved to the lusts of the body, they believe that man was born for the sake of these very things <note>This critiques the purely materialistic view that humans exist only to satisfy physical urges.</note>.

But those who are supported by the nature of God, O Tat—compared by the standard of their works—are held to be immortal rather than mortal. Having embraced with their intelligence all things on earth, in the sea, and whatever exists beyond the heavens, they raise themselves up so far as to behold the Good itself. Indeed, when they perceive this, they consider this life (which we consume here) to be a kind of misery. Despising all bodily things, they are carried toward the One alone.

This, O Tat, is how one merits the knowledge of divine things: if contemplation is the understanding of God, made possible by the presence of the divine Mixing-Bowl <term>Crater: a symbolic font of divine intellect sent by God to earth</term>.

**TAT.** Indeed, O father, I desire to be washed in the liquid of this Mixing-Bowl.

**TRISME.** <note>original: "TKISME."; likely a printer's error where a 'K' replaced the 'R'.</note> Unless you hate your body, my son, you cannot love yourself. But as soon as you love God instead of yourself, you shall immediately attain Mind; and having obtained this, you shall straightway acquire knowledge.

**TAT.** In what way do you assert these things, O father?

**TRISME.** It is impossible, my son, to attend to both at once—to the mortal and to the divine. For since only two things are found in the order of reality, the corporeal and the incorporeal—and the former is called mortal while the latter is divine—by the choice of one, we lose the other. Whenever the care for one is relaxed, the activity of the other is intensified. Therefore, the choice of the better path is honorable for the one who chooses; it not only renders the man who chooses divine, but also demonstrates piety toward God to others. However, the choice of the worse path destroys the man himself; yet against God, it commits no offense other than this one: that they pass through the middle like processions <note>original: "pompe per media trāseunt"; an image of empty spectacles or parades that have appearance but no spiritual substance.</note>.

<summary>Hermes explains the radical choice between the corporeal and the divine, asserting that one must look past physical desires to attain the "Mind" found in the divine Mixing-Bowl. He concludes that choosing the spiritual path divinizes the individual, while the material path leads to a hollow, fleeting existence.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Mind (Mens), Mixing-Bowl (Crater), Immortality, Dualism, Materialism, Contemplation, The Good</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Mercurius (Hermes Trismegistus) and his son/disciple Tatius. The previous page discussed the soul's duty to contemplate the divine and the "universal harmony" of the world. Here, the focus shifts to the necessity of transcending the physical realm to reach the "Monad" or ultimate Unity.</meta>

# I

They themselves are capable of no action and hinder others; in no other way do those people wander and stray in those processions because of the pleasures of the body. Since these things are so, O Tatius: divine duties ought to come first, and human ones should follow. God is surely beyond blame for the evils that come from us; for we prefer evil things to good ones. 

You see, O son, how we must transcend the celestial bodies, keep away from the choirs of <term>demons</term> <note>original: "demonum." In this context, these are not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spirits or planetary forces that govern the material world.</note> and the circuits of the stars, and surpass their movements, so that we may strive toward the one and only God. For the Good is insuperable: infinite without end, never beginning in itself, though having a beginning in relation to human knowledge. Yet such knowledge is not the start of the Good itself, but provides for us a starting point for knowing it. Let us therefore embrace this beginning. For when this is known, we shall travel through all things most swiftly. 

But it is difficult for one who leaves behind familiar and present things to turn oneself toward the higher and more powerful things. For those things we see with our eyes delight us too much; things that are hidden produce distrust. Moreover, the things that are visible are certainly evil; the Good is hidden from those who dwell upon manifest things. For it has no form, nor any shape. For this reason, it is like only unto itself, and truly unlike everything else. 

For indeed, the incorporeal cannot appear to the body; such is the difference between the like and the unlike, and the inferiority of the unlike to that which is like. The <term>Monad</term> <note>original: "Monas," meaning "Unity" or "The One." In Hermetic philosophy, it represents the indivisible source of all existence.</note>—that is, Unity—is the <term>principle</term> <note>original: "principium," meaning the fundamental source or first cause.</note>, root, and origin of all things. Without a principle, indeed, there is nothing. A beginning, however, is not a beginning of the principle, but of something else. 

The Monad, therefore, is the principle; it contains every number and is contained by none; it begets every number and is begotten by no number. Whatever is begotten is surely imperfect, divisible, growing, and diminishing. But to that which is perfect, none of these things happen. Indeed, that which increases, increases by the power of the Monad. But it vanishes through its own weakness, when it can no longer contain the Monad.

<summary>Mercurius explains to Tatius that reaching the one God requires transcending material pleasures and celestial influences. He introduces the "Monad" as the perfect, indivisible source of all numbers and existence, contrasting its eternal stability with the decaying nature of physical things.</summary>

<keywords>Monad, Unity, Tatius, Divine vs Human Duty, Principle, Incorporeal, Transcendence, Number Theory, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Mercurius (Hermes Trismegistus) and Tatius. The previous page concluded with the difficulty of the incorporeal appearing to the corporeal. This page introduces the famous metaphor of the "Crater" or Mixing Vessel and begins a new discourse on the paradoxical nature of God as both hidden and manifest.</meta>

->MER.<-
->V<-

These things, O Tatius, I offer according to my strength: let the image of God be inscribed within you. If you consider it diligently and know it with your internal eyes, believe me, my son, you will find the ascent to the heights. The image itself will lead you peacefully; for it possesses a certain vision, and it takes hold of those who burn with the desire to see, drawing them in the same way that the stone called a <term>magnet</term> <note>original: "magnes." The author uses the physical properties of magnetism—well known in the Renaissance—as a metaphor for the soul's natural attraction to the divine.</note> draws iron. The <term>Crater</term> <note>original: "Crater." This refers to the "Mixing Vessel" of the Divine Mind, a central concept in the *Corpus Hermeticum*. It represents a font of spiritual consciousness into which souls must "dip" to achieve enlightenment.</note> urges us to lay hold of wisdom; it is the wide-open vessel of the divine mind. Into this, whatever soul immerses itself, having been made a partaker of knowledge, it receives the mind. But indeed, so that the soul may raise itself to it, it must transcend the heavens and the circuit of the stars, surpassing their movements and leaving the <term>choirs of the blessed</term> <note>original: "beatorum choros." In Hermetic cosmology, these are the celestial orders or spirits dwelling in the spheres above the earth.</note> far below. Then it strives toward the one and only God: the insuperable and infinite Good without end. When the soul gazes upon this, it despises all bodies and considers this life (upon which we feed here) to be misery, being intently focused on the desire for the incorporeal Good alone. No one, however, is able to immerse themselves in the divine Mixing Vessel who does not hate their own body, and who does not love God Himself instead of themselves—and who, neglecting mortal things, does not cling to the divine alone, attending to it, meditating upon it, and burning for it. Soon the analogy of unity and numbers raises us to contemplate the true <term>Monad</term> <note>original: "monadem." The Monad is the philosophical term for the "One," the indivisible source from which all numbers and the entire universe originate.</note> and the true numbers of nature. Here ends *The Mixing Vessel*.

### ¶ Mercurius to Tatius his son: That God is at once hidden and manifest.

<margin>h</margin>
Furthermore, O Tatius, I will go through this discourse with you so that you may not lack the principal name, nor remain ignorant of that which seems hidden to many but is in fact clear. For if it were nowhere manifest, it would certainly be nothing. Whatever offers itself to the sight is <term>created</term> <note>original: "genitum." Literally "begotten" or "born." In Hermetic thought, anything visible belongs to the realm of "becoming" or creation, rather than eternal being.</note>. But that which is hidden is eternal. For there is no need for it to appear, since it never ceases to be. Indeed, it places other things before our eyes, but itself remains secret, as one that enjoys eternal life. For <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "phantasia." In this context, it refers to the faculty of perceiving appearances or mental images of the material world, which cannot grasp the uncreated God.</note> is concerned only with those things that are created, in which nothing exists except for the process of creation. But that unique uncreated thing is incomprehensible to imagination. Yet since all things become clear through Him...

<summary>Mercurius describes the "Crater" (Mixing Vessel) of Divine Mind that attracts souls like a magnet and explains that while God is hidden and uncreated, He is the source through which all created things become visible.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tatius, Crater, Mixing Vessel, Magnetism, Monad, Hidden God, Phantasia, Creation, Ascent of the Soul</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tatius from page 13. While the previous page discussed the soul's ascent and the "Crater" of the Mind, this page shifts to the manifestation of the invisible God through the visible, orderly structure of the physical universe.</meta>

# I

[God] shines again in all things and throughout all things. He appears especially to those to whom he has wished to communicate the knowledge of himself. You therefore, O my son Tatius, first of all beseech the Lord and Father—the Unique, the One, and the Source of the One <note>original: "vnicum/ vnum/ & a quo vnus." This refers to the Neoplatonic concept of the "Monad," where the ultimate God is the single source from which all unity and existence flow.</note>—with pious prayers, so that you might be worthy of his mercy. Thus, at last, you will be able to perceive so great a God, if even a single ray of his intellect should shine kindly upon you. For indeed, only hidden understanding perceives hidden things. Therefore, if you look with the eyes of the mind, O Tatius, he will be revealed to you (believe me). God is certainly free from all envy <note>original: "expers inuidie." This is a reference to Plato's *Timaeus*, suggesting that the Creator is not a jealous deity but one who wishes all things to be as much like himself as possible.</note>: he shines everywhere through every individual particle of the world; and he offers himself to be known by God <note>original: "a deo," perhaps meaning "divinely" or "from the divine source."</note> so that it is possible not only to understand him, but even (if I may say so) to touch him with your very hands. For his image presents itself everywhere before our eyes; it meets us and impresses itself upon us. But if he is hidden within you, how will you find either yourself or him? Finally, when you wish to see God, look up at the order of the remaining stars. Come now, who preserves their perpetual order? Indeed, every order of number is bounded by the limits of place. The Sun is the most excellent of the celestial gods. The remaining heavenly beings obey the Sun as if it were their prince and king. The Sun is so great—larger than both the earth and the sea—yet it allows the innumerable stars smaller than itself to revolve above it. Whom does he fear? Whom does he revere? O son, the movements of the stars are diverse. Who assigns the measure of motion to each? 

**TATIUS.** [What of] <term>Arcturus</term> <note>Arcturus is the brightest star in the northern constellation of Boötes. In ancient cosmology, its proximity to the celestial pole made it a symbol of the fixed, driving force of the heavens.</note>, which turns perpetually around the same point and pulls the whole <term>machine of the world</term> <note>original: "machina mundi." A common Renaissance term for the universe, viewing the cosmos as a complex, divinely-crafted mechanism or structure.</note>?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Who uses this instrument? Who circumscribed the sea within its borders? Who established the weight of the earth and balanced it in the center? There is surely someone, O Tatius, who is the author and lord of these things. For indeed, it is impossible to preserve place, or number, or measure without the power of an author. Order cannot arise from deformity; rather, deformity requires a lord who may provide order.

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus explains to Tatius that God, though hidden, is visible through the mathematical order and mechanical precision of the stars and the Sun. He argues that the complexity of the "machine of the world" necessitates a divine Author who maintains its measure.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tatius, divine order, celestial mechanics, Sun, Arcturus, machine of the world, Monad, creation, cosmological argument</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus (referred to here by the header "MER." for Mercurius) and his son Tatius. Following the previous discussion on the "Crater" of Mind and the presence of God in all things, Hermes now invites Tatius to contemplate the Divine through the grandeur of the cosmos and the intricate design of the human body.</meta>

## MER.

Would that the power <note>original: "facultas"</note> were given to you, O son, that with the aid of wings <note>original: "alarum adminiculo"</note> you might fly into the high regions of the air; and having obtained the middle region between heaven and earth, you would behold the solidity of the earth, the spreading of the sea, the flow of the rivers, the vastness of the air, and the swift, piercing speed of fire. O most happy spectacle, my son; O most blessed vision!

Indeed, with one movement of your eyes you would grasp the sequence of the whole world <note>original: "seriem mundi totius," referring to the Great Chain of Being or the orderly arrangement of the universe.</note>; and you would see the immobile Maker set in motion, hidden yet also clearly seen. But if you wish to investigate God the Artisan even through those fragile things sustained by the earth and hidden in the depths of the water: come now, son, look around at the workmanship <note>original: "opificium"</note> of the human body.

By its teaching, learn thoroughly: who is the maker <note>original: "conditor"</note> of so beautiful an image? Who is the painter of the eyes? Who turned the nostrils and ears as if on a lathe? Who spread the lips of the mouth? Who stretched and bound the nerves? Who irrigated the veins? Who piled up the solid bones? Who wove the flesh around with a thin skin? Who distinguished the fingers and joints? Who extended the foundations of the feet? Who pierced the pores and opened the passages? Who gathered and compressed the spleen? Who impressed the pyramid shape <note>In Renaissance anatomy, the heart was often described as a pyramid or cone-shaped organ.</note> upon the heart? Who then covered the fibers of the liver? Who carved the tubes <note>original: "fistulas," referring to the bronchial tubes or airways.</note> of the lungs? Who gave the belly its capacious size? Who fashioned the honorable members of the body in plain view? Who added the shameful parts in darkness <note>original: "obscena in obscuro," referring to the reproductive organs, traditionally kept hidden in anatomical and philosophical discourse.</note>, and willed that they lie secret from the sight of onlookers?

See how many works of divine art they demonstrate in one single matter; each one beautiful and rightly measured, and also differing from one another in their own duties. Who fashioned these individual things? What kind of mother? What kind of father? Was it not the invisible God himself alone, who has built all things <note>original: "cuncta"</note> by his own will <note>original: "propria voluntate"</note>? And since no one would dare to assert that a statue or an image could be made without a smith or a painter, shall we think that this marvelous constitution of the world was established without a Creator?

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus describes a mystical ascent to view the earth's elements and argues that the intricate, purposeful design of the human body proves the existence of an invisible Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Divine Artisan, anatomy, human body, teleology, Creator, elements, macrocosm, microcosm</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius) and his son Tat (Tatius). Having previously urged his son to see the Creator through the intricate design of the human body, Hermes now addresses the profound error of separating the creation from the Creator, expanding on the omnipresence and paradoxical nature of God.</meta>

[[header: I 15]]

O blind little man <note>original: "homunculum"; a diminutive used here to emphasize human frailty and spiritual blindness</note>: o too impious: o one overwhelmed by the deep darkness of ignorance. Beware, beware, I say, my son Tat: never deprive the work of the worker. Rather, having first invoked God by his fitting name, consider him to be the Father. For if you force me to speak something more bold: I shall say his essence is to conceive and to make all individual things. Just as nothing can be made without a maker, so it is impossible for God to exist always unless he is always acting upon all things <note>The Hermetic view posits that God's very being is defined by the continuous act of creation; if He ceased creating, He would cease to be.</note>—clearly in the heavens, the air, the earth, the sea, in the whole world, in every little particle of the world, both in that which exists and in that which does not yet exist. There is nothing in all nature that he himself is not. For he is what exists; he is also those things that do not exist at all. Those things which are, he has brought into the light; those things which are not, he hides within himself. This God is better than any name. He is hidden, yet again the most manifest of all. He is visible to the mind, yet present to the eyes. He is incorporeal, and yet—as I might say—**multi-corporeal** <note>original: "multicorporeus"; a term suggesting that while God has no physical body, all physical bodies together constitute his manifestation.</note>. For there is nothing in bodies which he is not, for he alone exists as all things. Moreover, he has all names, because he is the one Father; yet he has no name, because he is the Father of all.

Who then shall praise you: one placed above you or below you? Whither shall I turn my eyes to praise you: upward or downward, inward or outward? Is there any limit <note>original: "modus"</note> or place around you? Is there any other thing separate from all these? In you are all things; from you are all things. You provide all things, and yet you receive nothing in return. Indeed, you possess all things; however, that which you do not possess is itself nothing. When truly shall I praise you, Father? For we are not able to grasp your time and your moment. In what matter above all shall I sing your praises? Shall it be in those things you have created, or rather in those things you have not created? Shall it be perhaps in those things which you have brought forth into the light from the darkness, or in those which lie hidden as yet, stowed away in your secret bosom? Through what, finally, shall I dedicate my hymns to you? In such a song of praise <note>original: "laudatione"</note>, shall I exist as my own master, or...

[[vocabulary: little man, ignorance, craftsmanship, essence, multi-bodied, all things, moreover, song of praise]]

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus explains to Tat that God is the inseparable author of all existence, present in both the manifest and the hidden, and possessing every name while remaining nameless.</summary>
<keywords>Hermeticism, pantheism, divine essence, Tat, Mercurius, creation, multi-corporeal, divine praise</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discourse between Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius) and his son Tat, emphasizing God’s immanence in all things, and begins Book VI, a dialogue with Asclepius concerning the nature of the Good.</meta>

[[header: MERCURY]]

...how shall I become another <note>original: "tius alter efficiar?"; likely the conclusion of a rhetorical question asking how the speaker can be anything other than a reflection of the divine</note>? For you truly are whatever I am. You are whatever I do. You are, finally, whatever I say. For you are all things: nor is there anything else besides you. What, indeed, are you not? You are everything that has been begotten. You are indeed the understanding Father: the crafting God <note>original: "fabricans deus"; a reference to the Demiurge or the God who shapes the material world</note>. You are the efficient Good: making all things good. For the purest air is the soul of matter; the soul is the life of the air; the mind is the life of the soul; and finally, God is the life of the mind.

<margin>¶</margin> The fifth section <note>The translator or commentator is summarizing the fifth book/tractate of the Corpus Hermeticum</note> teaches that God is hidden and yet likewise shines through every single particle of the world. All things (which present themselves to us) are his images. Thus, the wonderful and beautiful order of things clearly declares the Ordainer: and individual things, circumscribed by their certain limits, declare their Lord and author. To Him, the sun—the king and prince of the stars—is obedient and subject: whom it fears and whom it reveres. In short, the entire structure of the world declares its artisan. Soon, he brings a more confident judgment concerning the divine internal generation: that it is the essence of God to conceive and make all things. So that for God to exist, unless he always acts upon all things, is impossible. For the divine Word <note>original: "logos"</note> is all things in such a way that it is no [single] thing of them all. In him, corporeal and incorporeal being and nothingness conspire and coincide into the same. Then Mercury, in songs of divine praise, finishes and ends the dialogue.

### <note>A pilcrow symbol (¶) marks the beginning of a new section.</note> That the Good is in God alone, and nowhere else; to Asclepius. VI

<margin>b</margin> The "One," O Asclepius, is found in nothing except in the one God; rather, the Good itself is God himself always. Wherefore the essence of all motion and knowledge must be God. Nothing is deprived of this essence: she <note>The Latin "essentia" is feminine.</note> possesses around herself a stable act, needing nothing, infinitely and superabundantly reaching out. That One is the principle of the universe: extending the Good and pouring out the Good. Whenever I name the Good: understand that which is the totality of good and that which is always good. This belongs to the unique God. For he is not in need of anything: so that by desiring to attain it, he might become evil. Nor is there ever any loss of anything in him: so that he might be affected by pain after a loss. For he is greater than any portion of evil. Nor is there anything stronger than him by which he might be conquered. No injury can fall upon him, by which he, irritated or provoked, might flare up in anger.

<summary>Hermes concludes his praise by describing God as the life-force of the mind and the soul, before beginning a new lesson for Asclepius on why absolute "Good" can only reside in the divine essence.</summary>
<keywords>Hermeticism, Panentheism, Asclepius, Divine Essence, Logos, The Good, Creation, Macrocosm</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Mercury (Hermes Trismegistus), shifting from the wonders of creation to a philosophical discourse on the nature of "The Good." Mercury is now addressing Esculapius (Asclepius) rather than Tat.</meta>

### MER

Nothing escapes his yoke, whereby he might be worthily despised and grow angry. Nor is anything wiser than he, such that it might strike rivalry in him. Therefore, since these things do not happen to him, nothing of his nature resists except the Good itself. Indeed, just as none of those things that are evil are in this essence, so the Good is found in nothing else. For in individuals, the rest are individual: both in small things and in great, and even in those which are according to the One, and in that very greatest and most powerful animal of all <note>Mercury refers to the Universe/Cosmos itself as a living, breathing "animal."</note>, which creation certainly fills with passions <term>passio: in this philosophical sense, it refers to "being acted upon" or undergoing change, rather than just emotion</term>. For generation <note>The process of being born or coming into existence.</note> is a certain passion. Where there is passion, there is in no way the Good. Where the Good is, no passion is placed. Where it is day, it is in no way night; where it is night, it is not even day. Therefore, the Good cannot exist in the process of generation. It remains, therefore, in that which is unbegotten. However, just as the participation of all things is granted to matter, so also by the participation of the Good, the world is good. I say "good" insofar as it also effects all things; in which part indeed the world is good; in all other things it is not good. For it is subject to passion and is mobile, and moreover, is the cause of all passions. In man, moreover, the good is defined by comparison to evil. Indeed, that which is not too evil, or even less evil, we call "good" in men. Thus it happens that our "good" is nothing other than the smallest portion of evil. Hence it also follows that a good of this kind cannot be separated from evil. For this good is stained by the admixture of evils; being tainted, it does not remain good any further. If it does not remain, it becomes evil. Therefore, the Good itself exists in God alone. Therefore, only the name of good is present to man, O Esculapius; the nature of the Good is not at all. For the material body cannot contain it, being constructed on all sides and oppressed by depravity, labors, pains, desires, wrath, deceptions, foolish opinions, and trifles. However, I judge the worst of all, O Esculapius, to be that each one of these things I have mentioned is believed to be the "highest good." In the first place, evil must be fled. Gluttony <note>original: *ventris luxus*, literally "the luxury of the belly."</note> is the fuel for all evils; here

<summary>Mercury explains that "The Good" exists only in God and the unbegotten realm, while the material world and humanity only experience "good" as a relative absence of evil or through participation in God's nature.</summary>
<keywords>The Good, God, Esculapius, Passions, Generation, Matter, Evil, Gluttony, Hermeticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Mercury (Hermes Trismegistus) to Esculapius (Asclepius). The previous page established that the "Good" cannot exist within the realm of generation or the physical world. Mercury now expands on this, arguing that the world is a collection of evils and that only God is truly Good.</meta>

### MER. Folio 17

...error; hence the privation of good. Indeed, I give great thanks to God, who poured into me this certain thought while I was meditating on the nature of the good: that good cannot exist in the world. For indeed, the world is a mass <note>original: "congeries"</note> of evils. But God is either of the Good, or is the Good itself: the exuberant fullness of God. It is certainly a pre-eminence of goods surrounding an essence that shines sincerely and most purely. Nor, perhaps, are these merely parts of the essence of God.

I assert this should be heard indeed, O Esculapius: that the essence of God (if God has any essence) is the Good itself. Truly, the Beautiful and the Good are not to be found in the world, nor in the parts of the world. For whatever things move the senses are but idols <note>original: "idola." In this philosophical context, these are not statues, but shadowy reflections or "phantoms" of reality that deceive the physical eyes.</note> and certain vain wonders. But those things which escape the organs of the senses belong to the Beautiful and the Good. Just as the sharpness of the eye does not discern God, so it does not recognize the Beautiful and the Good.

These indeed are parts of God: belonging to Him most fully, innate, inseparable, and especially beloved. And as God Himself loves these, so is God Himself loved by them. If you are able to perceive God, you will also perceive the Beautiful and the Good, shining through all things and illuminated by God. For Beauty is a good without comparison and without imitation. For God admits no comparison or imitation. 

Just as you will know God to be Beautiful and Good, so indeed it must never be claimed that other living things can be compared with God. If you seek God, you seek Beauty itself. What finally leads to it? There is a single way: piety joined with knowledge <term>cognitio: here referring to "Gnosis" or spiritual insight rather than mere factual learning</term>. Wherefore ignorant mortals, devious from the path of piety, do not fear to call even a man "good," although he can have no true knowledge of the good. Instead, he is ensnared and entangled in evils, and deems evil to be good, and uses evils incurably. He fears their loss and privation; finally, he strives with all machinations and strengths so that he is not only oppressed by a single evil, but indeed increases the number and magnitude of his evils. Such, O Esculapius, are the honors of m...

...lesser and good; which we are able neither to flee nor to hate. For this stands as the most difficult of all things, especially for this reason: because it is necessary for us to use them and feed upon them, and being deprived of them, we could in no way live our lives.

<margin>The sixth book <note>The marginal note refers to the division of the *Corpus Hermeticum*, indicating this section summarizes the core arguments of Book VI.</note> contends that only in God are the **Good** and the **nature of the Good** (that is, Goodness itself) found; likewise the nature of the Beautiful, the Powerful, and the Wise. In the world, however, there is only the *name* of good, and it proves that men are blinded by error. It warns us to abhor vices and teaches which path leads us to Goodness and Beauty itself—namely, **piety** joined with **knowledge**. Ignorant mortals, wandering from the path of piety, are never able to reach this. These are the words of the sixth book.</margin>

### That the greatest evil for men is to be ignorant of God. VII

<margin>q</margin> Where are you rushing, you drunken mortals? You who have drunk deep of the unmixed wine of ignorance; since you cannot bear it, vomit it out! Live soberly; look up with the **eyes of the mind** <note>original: "oculis mentis." This refers to intellectual or spiritual perception rather than physical sight.</note>. And if not all of you are able, let at least those do so who can. The plague of ignorance overturns the whole earth and corrupts the soul enclosed in the bonds of the body; nor does it allow her to take the path of salvation. Do not permit yourselves to be submerged in the lake of corruption and death.

Breathe now, breathe! Return to the fountain of life; lay hold of Him who will lead you into the **sanctuary of truth** <note>original: "adytum veritatis." An *adytum* was the innermost, most sacred chamber of a temple where only the initiated could enter.</note>. There is a shining light, mixed with no darkness. There, no one raves with drunkenness, but all are sober and watchful; and with the eyes of the mind, they gaze intently upon Him who wishes to be seen. He is neither perceived by ears, nor seen by eyes, nor expressed by speech; the mind alone beholds Him, the mind alone proclaims Him.

But first, you must strip off the garment you wear: the cloak of ignorance, the foundation of wickedness, the bond of corruption, the dark veil, the living death, the sensing corpse <note>original: "sensitivum cadaver." A provocative Hermetic description of the physical body as a "feeling" or "sensing" dead thing that traps the immortal soul.</note>, the walking tomb, and finally, the thief within your own house. While he flatters, he hates; while he hates, he envies. Such is the hostile shadow in which you are shrouded. It drags you down toward itself, lest perhaps...

...beholding the beauty of truth and the nearest good; lest you ever sense the depravity of this **stench** or these snares (which it constantly plots against you). This dulls and blunts the edge of the **inner senses**. Thick matter suffocates it: it intoxicates the soul with abominable and fastidious pleasure, so that you never hear, nor clearly see those things which by right ought to be heard, and especially to be looked upon.

<margin>The seventh book <note>original: "Septimus." This refers to the seventh treatise of the *Corpus Hermeticum*.</note> laments the **plague of ignorance**: which overturns the whole earth and corrupts the soul, enclosed in the bonds of the body. It exhorts the ignorant to return to their senses and commands us to strip off the **mortal garment** <note>The "mortal garment" is a common Hermetic metaphor for the physical body or the sensory passions that weigh down the soul.</note>: from which we are snatched headlong into ignorance and death. It seeks to recall us to the entry of the mind: which alone beholds Him who is perceived neither by ears, nor seen with eyes, nor brought forth by speech. Here ends the seventh.</margin>

### ¶ That none of the things that exist perish: but are rather changes, though deceived men call it "perishing." To Tatius. VIII

<margin>d</margin> Regarding the soul and the body, O son, we must speak: in what way the soul is immortal, and how great its power of acting is in the <term>concretion</term> <note>The joining together or solidifying of spirit into matter.</note> and the dissolution of the body. **Death** pertains to none of these. For *Thanatos* <note>original: "Thanatos," the Greek word for Death.</note>—that is, death—is indeed a concept of mortal naming: either something empty, or created by the removal of the first letter: *thanatos* <note>Death</note> instead of what is called *athanatos* <note>original: "athanatos," the Greek word for "Immortal." The author is using a wordplay to suggest that "Death" is just "Immortal" with the 'A' removed.</note>. For "Death" signifies perishing, but nothing of those things which are in the world perishes. For if the world is the **second god** <note>In Hermetic philosophy, the Cosmos is considered a living, divine being—the "Second God"—created by the "First God."</note> and an immortal living being, it is impossible for any part of an immortal living thing to perish. Whatever things are in the world are members of the world, especially man, the rational animal. The first of all is the truly eternal, immortal, and unbegotten God: the author of all. Second, thereafter, according to his image, is the **world** generated by him; and by that same God it is preserved, nourished, and gifted with immortality; living as if from its own father, it is always and is immortal...

<summary>This page concludes a warning against the "stench" of material ignorance and begins a dialogue with Tatius, arguing that death is a linguistic illusion and that nothing in the divine Cosmos truly perishes.</summary>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion between Hermes (Mercury) and his son Tatius from the beginning of Book VIII. Having established that nothing in the universe truly perishes, Hermes now distinguishes between the "eternal" nature of the Father and the "immortal" nature of the created Cosmos, explaining the mechanics of material dissolution and the unique status of Man.</meta>

[[header: I 18]]

...for it is indeed always living, and always immortal. Indeed, "always living" and "**eternal**" <note>original: "ſempiternum"</note> differ from one another. For that which is eternal has not been made by another; but if it was made by itself, it was not made by anyone else, because it is always. For the eternal is the whole, insofar as it is eternal; however, that Father is eternal of himself. But the **world** <note>In Hermetic thought, the "world" (*mundus* or *cosmos*) is often called the "second god."</note> was made ever-living and immortal by the Father.

And as much **matter** as had been subject to the Father, the Father himself, acting upon the corporeal and gathering it into a mass, rendered the whole **spherical** <note>The sphere was considered the most perfect and stable geometric form, reflecting the divine order of the universe.</note>, impressing quality upon matter that is immortal and possesses an eternal nature. 

Moreover, the Father, being full of all ideas, inserted qualities into the sphere: as if in a circle, he circumscribed it with every quality. He wished to adorn that which is after him <note>Referring to the created world as the successor or reflection of the divine.</note>, propping up every body with immortality, as it were, so that matter—should it wish to withdraw from this union—might not be dissolved again into its own **deformity**.

For when matter was incorporeal, O son, it was more deformed. This matter also possesses certain slight qualities, being subject to a revolving nature of growing and diminishing alike, which men call **death**. Confusion of this kind exists only around earthly living things. For the bodies of celestial beings preserve one and the same order, which they were assigned by their Father from the beginning. 

That order is preserved by the <term>restitution</term> <note>The process of returning to a prior state or the cyclical renewal of form.</note> of each indissoluble part. The restoration of the constitution of earthly bodies, and the dissolution itself, restores them into indissoluble and immortal bodies. And so there is a loss of sensation, not a destruction of bodies. 

The third living being is **man**, born in the image of the world, who, according to the Father's will, possesses—beyond all other earthly creatures—not only a kinship with the **second god** <note>The Cosmos or World-Spirit.</note>, but even an intelligence of the **first god**. For the senses perceive the second god, as he is corporeal; the first...

<summary>Hermes distinguishes between the self-existent eternal Father and the created immortal World, explaining that "death" is merely a transformation of matter rather than its destruction. He identifies Man as the third living being, uniquely possessing both a physical connection to the world and an intellectual connection to the Divine.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat regarding the immortality of the cosmos, transitioning into a summary of the eighth book's teachings. It then begins a new discourse addressed to Asclepius concerning the nature of sense, intelligence, and the relationship between the divine and human mind.</meta>

[[header: MERCURY]]

...but truly rises to God as an incorporeal and good **mind** <note>original: "mens"</note> through the mind. **TAT.** Therefore, this **living being** <note>original: "animal"; in this context, it refers to any "animated" or "living thing," including the cosmos itself, rather than just a beast.</note>: is it not destroyed? **TRISMEGISTUS.** Speak better, speak better omens, O son; and furthermore meditate on what God is, what the **world** <note>original: "mundus"</note> is, what an immortal living being is, and what a dissolvable living being is. Know also that the world exists from God and in God, but man exists from the world and in the world; however, the principle, comprehension, and constitution of all things is God.

<gloss>The Eighth</gloss> The Eighth book teaches primarily that celestial bodies constantly preserve one and the same course which they first received from their father. And that death and destruction appear first regarding earthly living bodies; but the name of death and the destruction of bodies is almost a hollow term. For bodies themselves are changed, but they do not perish. Rather, immortal **matter** <note>original: "materia"</note> exchanges the qualities themselves—those idols and images of the ideas with which God wished to adorn it. And man is an immortal living being but dissolvable, the bond and knot of universal nature, being partly corporeal and partly incorporeal; being capable of a higher mind, he is born in the likeness of the world so that through a sensible body he might comprehend the sensible world, and through his mind he might rise toward the best Mind by happy contemplation. This is the Eighth.

### ¶ That the Beautiful and Good are in God alone; but elsewhere, by no means. To Asclepius. VIII.

<margin>Regarding Sense</margin>
<gloss>Yesterday</gloss> Yesterday, O **Asclepius** <note>The legendary student of Hermes, associated with the Greek god of healing.</note>, I delivered a perfect discourse; but I judge it necessary at present that we should briefly discuss **sense** <note>original: "sensus"</note>. Sense and motion seem to differ in this most of all: because the latter is according to matter, but the former is according to essence. To me, however, both seem to coincide; nor are they distinguished in men by reason. In internal living creatures, sense is now united; but in men, it is 
<margin>Intelligence</margin>
**intelligence**. Indeed, intellect differs from intelligence just as God differs from **divinity**. For divinity proceeds from God; but intelligence proceeds from man. This intelligence is the sister of speech; or rather, both are instruments to each other. For neither is speech pronounced without intellection, nor does intellection come forth into light without speech. Wherefore sense...

<summary>Hermes explains to Tat that nothing truly perishes but only changes form, describing man as the "knot" between the corporeal and spiritual worlds. He then begins a lesson for Asclepius on how human intelligence and speech are divine instruments for understanding the cosmos.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Asclepius, immortality of matter, microcosm and macrocosm, intelligence vs intellect, nature of sense, divine essence</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the relationship between sense and intelligence, moving from the cosmological origins of the soul discussed on the previous page to the practical psychological and moral implications of how the mind receives divine or demonic influences.</meta>

[[header: I]]
[[page number: 19]]

...and intelligence in man: as if connected, they breathe together in turn. For we can neither understand without sense, nor can we perceive by sense in any way without understanding. However, it is possible to conceive of understanding without sense, in the manner of those who see phantasms <note>original: "phantasmata"; mental images or apparitions appearing in the mind's eye.</note> in dreams. Both operations also appear to such an extent in the external visions of dreams, yet sense is awakened from sleep into wakefulness. Furthermore, in the soul, the body and the man are united: whenever both parts of sense agree with each other, then the **understanding** <note>original: "intellectio"</note>, having been conceived, is arranged by the mind. The **Mind** <note>original: "Mens"</note> conceives all notions—the good ones indeed, whenever the seed is infused by God. But the contrary ones are conceived when seeds are cast by certain **demons** <note>original: "demonibus"; in Hermetic thought, these are intermediary spirits that can influence human thoughts and impulses.</note>. Truly, no part of the world is deprived of the presence of demons; their light descends entirely from God himself. Finally, a demon, having been poured into a man, scatters the seeds of its own notion. But the Mind, sprinkled with these seeds and pregnant, then gives birth to
<margin>Mind.</margin>
<margin>Demon.</margin>
adulteries, debaucheries, homicides, parricides, sacrileges, contempt for the divine, throat-cuttings, the overthrows of cities, plagues of men, and all other things which are the works of evil demons. Clearly, the seeds of God are few, but they are indeed great, beautiful, and good: namely, virtue, temperance, and **piety** <note>original: "pietas"</note>. Now, piety is the **knowledge of God** <note>original: "dei cognitio"</note>. He who recognizes God, being filled with all good things, attains divine notions—notions, I say, not like those of the many. For this reason, if any dedicate themselves to this knowledge: they neither please the common people, nor do the common people please them; they are thought to be insane and meet with laughter. Sometimes they are even held in hatred, afflicted with insults, and deprived of life. For we have said that wickedness dwells here, and that the earth is its province—I say the earth, not the whole world, as certain impious men falsely claim. Nevertheless, a man devoted to God, as soon as he has tasted the divine vision, consigns all other things to oblivion, and also those things which to other men are e[vil]... <note>The text cuts off mid-word "malorū" (evils).</note>
[[signature: c iij]]

**Vocabulary:** Mind, Demon, Understanding, Knowledge, Piety, Virtue, Temperance, Evil, World, Man.

<summary>This page explains that human understanding and sense are linked, but the Mind can be influenced by either divine seeds (virtues) or demonic seeds (vices). It defines piety as the true knowledge of God, which often leads the seeker to be misunderstood or persecuted by society.</summary>
<keywords>Hermeticism, Mind, demons, piety, divine knowledge, virtue, vice, perception, dreams</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the Hermetic dialogue regarding the influence of divine and demonic seeds on the human mind, transitioning into a cosmological description of the World as a living, sentient instrument of God's will.</meta>

HERMES
...are great: for to such a man, good things happen, as he deliberates prudently and refers each individual thing back to knowledge <note>original: "scientiam"</note>. And what is truly marvelous: he always turns even evils completely into good. But let us now return again to the discourse on sense. It is a human characteristic to join sense with understanding <note>original: "intellectio"; the faculty of the mind that processes sensory data into higher meaning.</note>. For all men (as I mentioned above) are guided by understanding. However, one man is guided according to matter, and another according to essence. For he who is a servant of depravity is material; he has also received the seed of understanding from demons (as we have said). But if any are masters of a goodness of soul, God himself makes use of their nature. For God is the author of all things: as the one who effects all things, he makes them similar to himself. Yet some good things, once generated, are sterile in their practical operation.

For the revolution of the world, agitating all generations, produces certain qualities—infecting some and settling them in evil, while defending others and purging them into goodness.
<margin>The World.</margin>
The World, O Asclepius <note>Asclepius is a frequent interlocutor in Hermetic dialogues, representing a student of the high mysteries.</note>, possesses sense and motion: not similar to human sense and motion, but certainly more powerful and far simpler. For the sense and intelligence of the world is this one thing: namely, to create and resolve all things. It is the instrument <note>original: "organum"</note> of the Divine Will, constructed for this primary reason: so that, receiving all seeds from God and hiding them in its whole bosom, it may produce all things by composing them, and again take all things away by dividing them. And like a skilled farmer, whatever has grown too large, the world prunes, so that at fixed intervals of time it may flourish again. 

Nor is there anything to which the world itself does not grant life. It is at once the place of life and also its establisher. Truly, bodies consist of matter in various distinctions: some of these are made of earth, some of water, others of air, and many also of fire, but all are certainly composite. Yet some are more densely packed, while others are simpler. The former are heavy, the latter light. Moreover, the velocity of its agitation, through its variety, induces the generation of quality. For its frequent breathing, being present, gives to bodies their qualities...

<summary>Hermes explains that the World is a living, sentient instrument of God's will that generates and recycles all life through the four elements. He distinguishes between material people influenced by demons and essential people who act as vessels for divine goodness.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, sense and intelligence, Divine Will, four elements, cosmology, seeds of God, demonic influence, composition and resolution</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the nature of the Cosmos and its relationship to the Divine, moving from the mechanisms of generation to the philosophical definition of God as the totality of all existence.</meta>

I                                                                                                   20

[...] it supplies them together with a superabundance of life. God, therefore, is the father of the Cosmos <note>original: "Cosmi"; the Greek term *Kosmos* implies both the universe and a sense of "order" or "beauty."</note>—that is, the father of the world. The Cosmos is the father of those things within the Cosmos, and the Cosmos is indeed the son of God. Those things which are in the Cosmos consist under the dominion of the Cosmos; and it is rightly called Cosmos <note>The text plays on the etymology: in Greek, *Kosmos* means both "the world" and "ornament" or "order."</note>. For it adorns all things with a variety of generation, and with the unceasing operation of life, the perpetual speed of necessity, the mixing of elements, and the order of those things produced. The Cosmos itself, therefore—meaning "the Ornamented"—is named by necessity and merit: it is the sense and intelligence of all living things, which flow into it from external sources, since it contains within itself the things inspired by God.

The Cosmos perpetually preserves whatever it received from God from its very origin. God, certainly, is not—as it seems to some—lacking in sense and mind. For those who speak such errors are oppressed by misery. Whatever things exist, O Asclepius <note>Asclepius is the student to whom Hermes Trismegistus is speaking.</note>, are both in God and dependent upon God: some acting through the body, some moving through the animal essence <note>original: "essentiam animalem"; this refers to the 'soul-force' that gives life and movement to creatures.</note>, others giving life through the spirit, and others being the receptacles of the dead. But we shall say more correctly that God does not *have* such things; but rather, to express the clear truth, we shall confess that he *is* all things. Nor does he receive these things from outside sources; nevertheless, he extends himself toward the outermost things. This is the sense and intelligence of God. Therefore, there shall never be a time in which anything that exists vanishes into nothingness. Whenever I say "existing things," I am speaking of God’s treasure-house. For God himself embraces all existence. Outside of him is nothing. Outside of "nothing" is he himself. 

These things, O Asclepius, will seem true to you if you understand them, but unbelievable to the ignorant. For to understand is to believe. But not to believe is, without a doubt, to be ignorant. <margin>To believe is to understand.</margin> Indeed, my discourse has run all the way to the truth. The mind, being vast and guided by discourse to a certain conclusion, has reached the truth; finally comprehending all things and finding them in harmony with those things which in—

[Modern metadata at the bottom of the page: Early European Books, Copyright © 2011 ProQuest LLC. Images reproduced by courtesy of the Biblioteca Nazionale Centrale di Firenze. CFMAGL. 1.6.404]

<summary>Hermes explains the relationship between God, the Cosmos, and all living things, arguing that God is the totality of existence rather than a separate being who merely "possesses" traits. He concludes that understanding these mysteries is the same as having faith in them.</summary>
<keywords>Cosmos, Asclepius, Divine Intelligence, generation, essence, existence, faith and understanding, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the Hermetic dialogue, transitioning from the end of the Ninth Book (concerning intelligence and the "seeds" of the soul) into the Tenth Book, titled "The Key," addressed to Hermes's son, Tatius.</meta>

MFR. <note>These initials likely refer to the editor or a specific section marker in the Renaissance edition.</note>

...interpreted, he immediately believed, and in that very grace of faith, he happily rested. Therefore, regarding divine matters: once they are understood, they are indeed believed; but when they are not understood, they are denied. Let these remarks concerning intelligence suffice.

### The Ninth Book instructs men.
While all men make use of intellection, those who are **material** conceive from the seeds of demons <note>original: "demonum spermatibus"; in this context, "demons" refers to lower spirits or planetary influences that tether the soul to physical desires.</note>, whose presence is found in every part of the world. Being pregnant with these, they eventually give birth to adulteries, debaucheries, homicides, parricides, and every kind of disgraceful act. On the other hand, **essential and spiritual men** conceive every ornament of virtue, with God acting as the sower; they bring forth every good office of virtue. For the seeds of God are few, yet they are great, beautiful, and good, and far superior to the innumerable multitude of vices. 

Those who pursue these seeds do not please the common crowd, nor does the crowd please them; indeed, they are thought to be insane and are met with laughter, because the earth is the seat and province of wickedness. The World, however, was formed by this law: that by receiving all seeds from God, it would become a worthy instrument of divinity, and all things would proceed toward their birth in the wondrous beauty of a fair order. In this order they should persevere until they finally reach their end. This would surely happen, were it not that *an enemy coming in the midst of the wheat sowed tares* <note>A direct allusion to the Parable of the Tares in Matthew 13:25, showing how Renaissance translators blended Hermetic philosophy with Christian scripture.</note>. You, therefore, having become spiritual, choose the divine seeds and cautiously flee now from the seeds of evil demons (who watch to occupy us with material affection while we sleep). This ends the ninth book.

# ¶ The Key of Hermes Trismegistus to Tatius. X.

I addressed yesterday’s discourse to you, O Asclepius <note>Asclepius was a primary student of Hermes; here the focus shifts to Tatius, Hermes's son.</note>. Whence it is right to dedicate today’s to **Tatius**, especially since this present disputation will be a kind of compendious summary of all the things we have spoken of previously. 

God, and the Father, and the Good, O Tatius, have the same nature—or rather, the same activity. For the terms "increase" and "decrease" apply to those things which move between the mutable and the immutable, that is, between the human and the divine. He wills each of these to exist. Elsewhere, however, we have demonstrated the activity of divine and human things <note>original: "actum... diuinorum atq; humanorũ"</note>, which must be understood in this context. The **will** of God is His activity; His **essence** is to will that all things exist. What is God?

<summary>This page concludes a discourse on how "divine seeds" produce virtue while "demonic seeds" produce vice, before beginning 'The Key,' a summary of Hermetic philosophy addressed to Tatius.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tatius, seeds of virtue, demons, divine will, essence, The Key, Asclepius</keywords>

...and father and the Good: unless He is the very bond <note>original: "nodum"; the "knot" or "nexus" that holds all things together</note> of all these existing things. Moreover, the existence of these things depends on Him: He is God, He is the Father, He is the Good, to whom nothing else from all things can be applied. For the World and the Sun are fathers to those things that exist through participation <note>In Hermetic philosophy, secondary beings like the Sun "participate" in God's power to create, acting as secondary causes</note>, yet they are not the cause of life and goodness to living things. If this is the case, then everything is moved by the will of the Good, without which nothing can ever exist or come to be. As a father of children, He undertakes both procreation and nourishment solely through the appetite for the Good (for the Good is active); this cannot belong to anyone but Him. Since He receives nothing, He wills all things to be. I do not say, O Tat, that He creates all things. For a creator, failing over a long period of time, is a concept unworthy of Him. If He were to create at one time and cease at another, He would be deficient in both quantity and quality. Sometimes He would arrange things of a certain quality or quantity, and at other times their opposites. But God is both Father and the Good, by the very fact that He is all things. It is His nature to command these things; indeed, He wills them to be, and they are. And He is especially Himself: for all other things exist because of Him. It is truly the property of the Good, O Tat, to make itself known. That is what the Good is, O Tat.

**TAT.** Father, you have made us participants in a vision of the Good and the Beautiful. By such a vision, the eye of my mind has now been purified.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** It is not at all like a ray of the sun, which, with too much brightness, shocks the eyes and makes them grow dim; the sight of the Good is not like that. Rather, it illuminates and increases the light of the eye to the extent that one is able to receive the influx of the intelligible splendor <note>original: "intelligibilis splendoris"; this refers to a spiritual light that can only be perceived by the intellect, not the physical eyes</note>. This divine light is swift and sharp in its penetration, filling every thing with innocence and immortality. Those who are able to drink more deeply of this brilliance from the body often fall into a sleep <note>This refers to a divine trance or "ecstasis" where the physical senses are suspended so the soul can see</note>, passing from the body to that most beautiful sight—just as **Caelus** and **Saturn**, our ancestors, did.

<margin>Caelus</margin>
<margin>Saturn</margin>

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus explains to Tat that God is the "bond" of all existence and the ultimate source of the Good. He describes the Divine Vision as a spiritual light that purifies and strengthens the soul's "eye" rather than blinding it like physical sunlight.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Tenth Book of the Corpus Hermeticum, "The Key." Hermes (identified here by the Latin "Mercurius") continues his instruction to Tat regarding the path to divine Union and the nature of the soul's transmigration.</meta>

->HERMES<-

...creators <note>original: "tores," likely "auctores"; refers to the secondary causes or celestial powers mentioned on the previous page</note> arise. TAT: I wish that we also could, father. HERMES: I wish it were so, my son. Yet we are still too weak for such a sight. For we shall be able to lift the eyes of the mind and behold that incomprehensible beauty and goodness only when we have said nothing at all about it. For the knowledge of it is **divine silence** <note>original: "divinum silentium"; a core Hermetic concept where God is known through the cessation of human speech and thought</note> and the intent application of all the senses. He who understands this can think of nothing else. He who beholds it can behold nothing else besides it. He who hears it can hear nothing else after it, nor can he even move the members of his body. Truly released from all bodily senses and movements, he rests motionless. For that which illuminates the whole also shines through the entire mind, draws the whole soul away from the body, and finally transforms it entirely into the **essence of God** <term>theosis: the process of a human being becoming divine or God-like through spiritual transformation</term>. For it is impossible, my son, for a human soul lying in the dregs <note>original: "fece"; literally the dregs or sediment at the bottom of a liquid, used here to describe the material world</note> of the body to take on a divine form. Nor is it permitted to gaze upon the beauty of God unless one has first been transformed into God.

TAT: In what way do you say this, O father? HERMES: It is the distribution of every soul, my son. TAT: And in what manner do you distribute the changes within? HERMES: Have you not heard in those things we spoke of generally, how from the **one soul of the whole world** <term>Anima Mundi: the "World Soul," an intrinsic connection between all living things and the divine</term>, all souls flow through the entire universe as if distributed and running about? Certainly, the changes of these souls are many; some indeed for the better and more fortunate, but others toward the contrary. For some of the reptiles change into...

</margin>This is certainly the judgment and the punishment of the evil soul. The vice of the soul is ignorance. For a soul that is ignorant of the nature of things and of the Good—being blinded—is entangled in the passions of the body. A soul corrupted by the influence of a *cacodemon* <note>original: "cacodemonis"; an evil spirit or malevolent entity that draws the soul away from divinity</note> (that is, an evil spirit), ignoring its own true self, serves the cast-off body and deformed monsters. It carries its own body around like a hostile burden; it does not rule the body, but instead is subjugated by it through ignorance. On the contrary, the virtue of the soul is knowledge. For the person who is truly learned is good, pious, and already divine.

TAT: Who is such a person, O father?

TRISMEGISTUS: One who neither speaks much nor hears much. For one who devotes himself to two discourses—whether in the speaking or the hearing of them—fights in the darkness. For God, the Father, and the Good, is neither pronounced by the tongue nor perceived by the ears. Since these things are so in all that comes from Him (because they cannot exist apart from Him), the senses are present within them. Yet there is a great difference between knowledge and sense. For sense is the movement of that which prevails; knowledge is the goal of science. Science is the gift of God. For every science uses the incorporeal mind as an instrument, just as the mind uses the body. Therefore, both return into bodies, both the intelligible <note>original: "intelligibilia"; things that can be understood by the mind but are not physical</note> and the material. For it is necessary that all things consist of opposition and contrariety; it is impossible for it to be otherwise.

TAT: Who then is this material god?

TRISMEGISTUS: The world. It is beautiful, yet it is not good. For it is composed of matter and is subject to passions. It is the first of those things that suffer. It is the second of the things that exist. Furthermore, it is lacking by its very nature; though created at a specific time, it exists forever in a state of becoming. It is born as the offspring of all qualities and quantities. For it is mobile. Every material motion must be called a "generation" <note>original: "generatio"; in this context, the process of coming into being or physical change</note>. In this way, the intelligible state drives material motion. Since the world is a sphere—that is, a head—nothing material exists above it...

<summary>Hermes explains that ignorance is the soul's greatest vice and punishment, while knowledge leads to divinity. He defines the physical world as a "material god" that is beautiful but inherently imperfect and subject to change.</summary>
<keywords>ignorance, cacodemon, virtue, knowledge, material god, cosmos, sense vs. science, Hermes Trismegistus, Tat</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the Tenth Book of the Corpus Hermeticum, "The Key," where Mercurius (Hermes Trismegistus) describes the hierarchical structure of the cosmos and the human being, specifically the relationship between Mind, Soul, Spirit, and Body.</meta>

MERCURIUS:
Just as there is nothing intelligible <note>In this context, "intelligible" refers to the higher realm of pure thought and spirit, as opposed to the "sensible" or material realm.</note> beneath our feet, so too are all things here below material. The Mind, however, moves the head in a circle—that is, a motion according to the very nature of the head. Therefore, whatever things are adjacent to the membranes of that head—which is truly the soul—are born immortal; it is as if the body were situated within the soul. Those things which possess a soul are filled with the body. However, whatever is far from the membrane where they reside—those which participate more in the soul—exists as a body. Yet the whole is a living being <note>original: "animal"; in the Hermetic sense, an "animal" is any ensouled or living entity, including the world itself.</note>.

Thus, the world is composed of both a certain material part and an intelligible part. The world is the first living being; man is the second living being after the world, but the first among all other living creatures. Whatever gift of soul is granted to others, man also possesses. He is weighed as being not only not good, but even evil, insofar as he is mortal. For the world is not "good" because it is subject to motion; yet it is not "evil," since it is immortal. Man, however, because he is both subject to motion and mortal, is judged to be evil.

The soul of man is carried in this manner: the Mind is in reason; reason is in the soul; the soul is in the spirit <note>original: "spiritu" or pneuma; a subtle, airy substance thought to bridge the gap between the immaterial soul and the physical flesh.</note>; and the spirit is in the body. The spirit, diffused through the veins, arteries, and the blood, moves the living being in every part; it sustains and carries about the mass of the body which is suspended within it. 

Because of this, some have been deceived into thinking that the fluid of the blood is the soul. These people were clearly unaware of the truth: because first, it is necessary for the spirit to flow back toward the soul; then the blood must coalesce and the hollow veins and arteries must be extended; finally, the living being is dissolved—and that is the death of the body.

All things, however, depend on one Principle; and that Principle comes from the One and Only. This Principle is indeed moved so that it may again exist as a Principle. Yet the One itself remains fixed; it does not depart from its Unity. Therefore, these three are: God the Father (the Good), the World, and Man. God holds the world; the world holds man. The world is the son of God; man is the offspring of the world. For God does not ignore man, but has care for him, and He wishes to be known by him.

<summary>Mercurius describes the hierarchy of the human constitution (Mind, Reason, Soul, Spirit, and Body) and the relationship between the three great entities: God, the World, and Man.</summary>
<keywords>Mind, Soul, Spirit, Pneuma, Trinity, Macrocosm, Microcosm, Mortality, Immortality, Principle of the One</keywords>
