This is the ascent to Olympus <note>A classical reference used here to signify the celestial spheres or the dwelling of the Divine.</note>. From this alone the soul becomes good—not merely good at some times and bad at others by necessity, but consistently good.

TAT: How can you assert this, my Trismegistus?

TRISMEGISTUS: I assert this, I say: that the soul of a child contemplates itself, not yet having departed from its own nature because of the body—for the body is not yet fully formed—and it sees itself as beautiful from every side. For it is not yet corrupted by the passions of the body, being at that time dependent on the Soul of the World <note>original: "mundi totius anima"; the "Anima Mundi" is the vital force that permeates and animates the entire universe.</note>.

But when the body is fully formed, it disperses and draws the soul into its own heavy mass; then the soul is subjected to forgetfulness and is deprived of the vision of the Beautiful and the Good. This forgetfulness is itself the essence of wickedness. The same occurs to those who depart from the body. For when the soul retreats into itself, the spirit <note>original: "spiritus" or pneuma; a subtle, airy substance that connects the immaterial soul to the physical blood.</note> is contracted into the blood, and the soul into the spirit. The Mind, being divine and free from its veils, and having by its very nature obtained a fiery body <note>A subtle "astral" vehicle made of celestial fire, which allows the Mind to travel through the cosmos.</note>, wanders through all places; it leaves the soul to a just judgment and a deserved punishment.

TAT: In what way do you say this, O Father?

TRISMEGISTUS: The Mind is separated from the soul, and the soul from the spirit. The soul is the garment of the Mind. The spirit is the garment of the soul. You must understand this along with me, O son, as I speak, and harmonize your thoughts; the listener must have a hearing sharper than the voice of the speaker.

The wrapping of these garments, O son, is completed in the earthly body. For indeed, it is utterly impossible for the Mind, naked in its own nature, to reside in an earthly mass. For the earthly dregs are not able to receive such a divine Mind, nor can a suffering body sustain so great a divinity. Therefore, the Mind took the soul as a cloak. Furthermore, the soul, being divine, uses the spirit as its vehicle. The spirit runs through the whole living being.

And so, as soon as the Mind is released from the earthly body, it immediately puts on its own proper cloak—namely, a fiery body. As long as it is wrapped in this celestial fire, it cannot swell into or be confined by an earthly body. For the Earth...

<summary>Hermes explains that the soul is pure in infancy but becomes "corrupted" by the body's passions and forgetfulness. He describes the Mind, Soul, and Spirit as a series of nested "garments" or vehicles required for the divine Mind to inhabit a physical body.</summary>
<keywords>Gnosis, Anima Mundi, forgetfulness, garments of the soul, fiery body, pneuma, Tat, Trismegistus</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the Tenth Book of the Corpus Hermeticum, "The Key." The previous page discussed the soul's descent into the body and the loss of divine vision through the "heavy mass" of matter. Here, Hermes (Mercurius) explains the elemental protection of the soul and the specific punishments for impiety.</meta>

**MERCURIUS**

Indeed, the soul cannot endure fire; for it would be entirely consumed by even a tiny spark. For this reason, moisture is spread around the earthly mass <note>original: "terrene moli"; referring to the physical body as a heavy, material weight.</note> as a sort of barrier against the combustion of fire. Since the Mind is the sharpest and swiftest of all divine conceptions, it encompasses the bodies of each of the elements. For the very Architect of the heavens uses fire above all for His craftsmanship. While the Maker of the whole uses all things, every earthly craftsman uses only those things found upon the earth. For the mind of man, being deprived of divine fire, is fit only for human dispositions and cannot construct divine things.

The human soul—not every soul, indeed, but the pious and blessed one—is divine. After such a soul has been released from the prison of the body through death, having submitted to all the commands of virtue and piety, it certainly becomes either Mind or God <note>This reflects the Hermetic doctrine of "apotheosis" or "deification," where the purified soul regains its original divine status.</note>. 

The struggle of religious piety is this: to acknowledge God and to do harm to no one. However, the impious soul remains in its own nature and torments itself greatly; it seeks an earthly and human body to enter. For no body other than a human one can contain a human soul; nor is it right for a rational soul to fall into the body of an animal lacking reason. For divine law prohibits such a wicked form of rebirth <note>original: "generationem tam nefariam"; Hermes here rejects the idea of "metempsychosis" or "transmigration" into animals, a point where Hermeticism often differed from certain Pythagorean or Platonic schools.</note>.

**TAT.** 
In what way then, O father, is the soul of a man tortured, and what punishment is more grievous for such a soul?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** 
Impiety, my son Tat. For what fire has a more burning flame than that of impiety? What biting wild beast so tears the body as impiety mangles the soul? Do you not see how many evils weigh down the impious mind? "Alas!"—so cries the impious soul—"I burn! I am consumed! I know not what to do! Evils rushing in from every side devour me, miserable as I am! I see nothing, alas, wretched me, I hear nothing!" 

Such are the voices of afflicted souls; such is the punishment fitting their nature—not such as you perhaps, my son, and certain others might imagine, who believe our soul puts off its human form after death...

to degenerate into the bodies of brutes. For this is a wicked 
error: yet the manner of its punishment is different. The Mind, 
when it becomes a demon <note>In this context, a "demon" (daimon) is not a malevolent being, but a powerful intermediary spirit or a state of heightened spiritual agency.</note>, is commanded to take up a fiery 
body for the service of God. Thence, having entered into a wicked 
soul, it lashes it with the whips of its sins. The wicked 
mind, lashed by these, turns itself toward murders, insults, 
reproaches, and various robberies: finally, it sinks into 
all those things through which men commit crimes. But 
when the Mind flows into a holy soul, it raises it up 
to the light of wisdom. Such a soul thereafter 
never languishes in the stupor of sleep, but brings 
help to the human race through both words and works, and 
assists in every way, always imitating its parent <note>The "parent" refers to God, the Creator or Father of the All.</note>. 
Therefore, O son, it is necessary for us, giving thanks to 
God, to pray that we might be made partakers of the good Mind; 
for the soul migrates into a better state, but never into a worse. 
There is also a certain communion of souls: the souls 
of the gods communicate with the souls of men. God 
shows Himself as common to each one. For He is more 
excellent than all things, and all things are weaker than He. 
Let the world be subject to God, man to the world, 
and the brute animal to man. God is above 
all things and around all things. The rays of God are His 
actions; the rays of the world are the forces of nature; 
the rays of man are the arts and sciences. Action 
is exercised through the world, and these actions descend upon man 
through the natural rays of the world: nature through the 
elements, and man through the arts and sciences. 
This administration of the entire world depends upon 
the nature of the One, and rightly runs through one matter: 
than which nothing is more powerful, nothing more divine, 
and finally, nothing more unified. The communion 
of men is with the gods, and the communion of the 
gods is with men. This is the Good Demon <note>original: "bonus demon"; referring to the Agathos Daimon, the personification of divine intellect or a guiding "Good Spirit" that brings enlightenment.</note>. 
The soul that is full of this is blessed; but the one 
that is empty is wretched. **TAT.** For what reason 
do you say this, O father?

<meta>This page continues the Tenth Book of the Corpus Hermeticum, titled "The Key." # MERCURIUS TRISMEGISTUS

**Know, my son, that every soul has the Good itself for its Mind.** <note>In Hermetic thought, "Mind" (Latin: *Mens*, Greek: *Nous*) is not just intellect, but a divine spark or "light" that connects the human to the divine.</note> For it is of this Mind that our current discourse concerns, not of its minister—of whom we spoke before—who is sent down to the lower realms <note>original: "inferos"</note> by judgment. A soul deprived of the presence of the Mind is capable neither of acting nor of speaking anything. For often, indeed, the Mind exists outside the soul; at which time the soul neither hears nor sees, but is like an unreasoning animal. Such is the great power of the Mind: it deserts a soul of this kind when it is entangled in the body and dragged by that body toward the lower depths.

Such a soul, my son, has no Mind; and therefore it is not right to call such a being a "man." For **man is a divine animal**, and is not to be compared with the brutes of the earth, but with the celestial gods. Indeed, if one must dare to speak the truth: a true man is either superior to the heavenly inhabitants, or at least shares an equal lot with them. For whichever of the heavenly beings descends to earth must leave the boundary of heaven; man, however, ascends into heaven and measures it. Nothing escapes him, whether in the depths or the heights, and he diligently inquires into all other things. What is even greater: without even leaving the earth, he is raised up into heaven; so vast is the power of human nature.

Therefore, one must dare to say: **a man of the earth is a mortal god, while a god of the heavens is an immortal man.** <note>This is one of the most famous aphorisms in the Hermetic tradition, asserting the essential identity and dignity shared between humanity and divinity.</note> Thus, by the power of these two—namely, man and the world—all individual things are governed. Finally, all things are subject to the One.

He <note>This likely refers to the author Hermes or the commentator's interpretation of the text.</note> constantly asserts that God is absolutely all things, which individual things contract through a unique participation. Nevertheless, God is the Father and the Good by the very fact that He is all things. Soon he discusses the rapture of the soul in silence, where those things seen with the eyes of the mind are neither lawful to reveal, nor is the power of revealing them granted. 

As for what he adds regarding the transformation of animals, the following discourse of Mercurius declares that one should follow not a literal fiction of words, but rather an **allegory of fiction**, <note>The author is explaining that when ancient texts speak of humans becoming animals, they are speaking symbolically of the soul losing its reason, not a physical metamorphosis.</note> when he says: "For no body other than the human one can hold a human soul; nor is it lawful for a rational soul to fall into the body of a soul lacking reason, for divine law prohibits a generation so wicked." And a little after...

<summary>Hermes explains the divine nature of man, famously describing man as a "mortal god" capable of reaching the heavens. He also clarifies that "metempsychosis" (humans turning into animals) is a symbolic allegory, as divine law forbids a rational soul from entering a mindless beast.</summary>
<keywords>Mortal God, Human Nature, Mind (Mens), Celestial Gods, Divine Law, Metempsychosis, Allegory, Governance of the World</keywords>

The text refers to the common misunderstanding of metempsychosis, or the transmigration of souls into animals.</note>, which causes a soul to be a child to itself while contemplating itself; let those who defend the hidden mind and intelligence see to this. ### THE KEY

## XI. THE MIND TO HERMES

**Cease delaying for so long, O Thrice-Greatest Hermes.** Be silent now; remember what has been said before. Indeed, I will not be reluctant to express my meaning to you—all the more because many and various opinions regarding the world and God wander among mortals.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Indeed, to frankly confess the truth of this matter: I do not yet hold it myself. Therefore, my Lord, express the pure truth; for I trust that you alone are able to elucidate such things.

**THE MIND:** Hear me then, O son. God, Eternity, and the Universe relate to each other in this way: **God is Eternity; Time is Generation.** <note>The term "Generation" (Latin: *generatio*) refers to the process of coming into being, birth, and the physical cycle of life and death.</note> God produces Eternity; Eternity produces the World; the World produces Time; and Time produces Generation.

The essence, as it were, of God is: the Good, the Beautiful, Blessedness, Wisdom, and Eternity. The essence of the World is order; the essence of Time is change; and the essence of Generation is life and death. The acts of God are Mind and Soul. Eternity consists of perseverance and immortality; the institution of the World and the restoration of Time; growth and diminution; and finally, the quality of Generation.

Therefore, **Eternity is in God; the World is in Eternity; Time is in the World; and Generation is in Time.** Eternity stands around God. The World moves in Eternity. Time is bounded in the World. Generation is completed in Time. Perhaps, therefore, God is the essence of all; but the essence of Eternity is the World.

The work of Eternity is the World, which was not made at a specific moment in time, but was always made from the Age <note>original: "euo" (aevum). In Hermetic thought, the *Aevum* is a state of "ever-present" time that stands between human linear time and God's absolute eternity.</note>. Since the Age never ceases, the World will also never cease. And since the World is contained within Eternity, no part of the World shall perish.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** But what is the Wisdom of God?

**THE MIND:** It is the Good, the Beautiful, Blessedness, every virtue, and the Age; from which immortal...

<summary>This page concludes "The Key" by dismissing the idea of human souls reincarnating as animals and begins a new discourse on the cosmic hierarchy of God, Eternity, the World, Time, and the cycle of Generation.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, The Mind, Nous, Eternity, Aevum, Generation, Cosmology, Metempsychosis, Essence of God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of "The Mind to Hermes" from Book XI. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, detailing the ontological hierarchy where God sustains Eternity, and Eternity sustains the World.</meta>

->MER<-

...immortality <note>original: "talitatē," completing the word "immortalitatem" from the previous page.</note>, Eternity <note>original: "euū" / "aevum." In this context, Aevum refers to a type of "ever-time" or eternal duration that bridges the gap between the timelessness of God and the linear time of the physical world.</note> provides endurance, granting perseverance to matter. For the origin of matter depends on Eternity, just as Eternity depends on God.

Generation and Time are twin natures; they exist both in heaven and on earth. In heaven, these two are surely immutable and immortal, but on earth, they are mutable and subject to corruption.

<margin>The Soul of the World.</margin>
The soul of Eternity is God; the soul of the world is Eternity; and the soul of the earth is heaven. God is in the Mind; the Mind is in the soul; and the soul is in matter. All these things exist through Eternity. This whole body, in which all bodies are contained, is a soul full of Mind and God; she fills the innermost parts of the world and embraces its outermost limits, providing life to all things. From the outside, she provides for this great and perfect living creature, the world; from the inside, she provides for all other living beings that dwell within the world.

Above in heaven—which remains forever the same—she holds all things together; below on earth, she stirs up Generation. Eternity certainly contains the world, whether by necessity, providence, or nature. And if anyone thinks of anything else, or ever will think of it, God is the one who does it all.

The action of God is an insuperable power, with which no one would dare to compare any human or divine power. Therefore, take care, O Mercury <note>original: "Mercuri." The speaker addresses Hermes Trismegistus by his Roman name, Mercury.</note>, that you do not consider anything of the lower or even of the higher realms to be like God, for you would wander far from the truth. For nothing is like Him, who is unique, alone, and One. Do not think that the same power belongs to anyone else but God.

For what after Him is such, regarding both immortality and the transmutation of quality? What else besides this will God effect? For God is not **idle** <note>original: "ociosus." In Hermetic philosophy, God is defined as "Pure Act," meaning He is never resting but always in the process of creating and sustaining.</note>; for if He were, all things would be idle. Since all things are full of God, idleness is found in no part of the world. Indeed, idleness is a hollow term, both regarding the one who acts and that which is acted upon. It is necessary that all things be made, and always be made according to the nature of each location. The Actor is not present to one thing only, but to all. He does not produce only one thing, but produces the universe. For being a power effective in itself, it is not at all insufficient in those things which are made. For under it are the works...

<summary>The Mind explains the cosmic hierarchy, stating that God sustains Eternity, which in turn sustains the world and life. The text emphasizes God's constant activity, arguing that idleness is impossible in a universe filled with the divine presence.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Aevum, Eternity, Generation, Soul of the World, God as Actor, cosmic hierarchy, immortality, Mercury</keywords>

Truly, all living bodies are animated <note>original: *animata*, meaning they possess a soul or *anima*.</note>; however, non-living things are almost entirely bare. </margin> ...be present; give yourself to me: you will immediately understand the work of God. It was necessary for this work to be present so that there might be those things which are made, those which were being made, and those which will be made in the future. That, oh most sweet Mercury <note>original: *mi Mercuri*. The text refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage whom the Romans identified with Mercury.</note>, is life; that is indeed beautiful; that is good; and that is, finally, God.

But if you ask that I place these things before your eyes through examples of the work: observe what happens to you when you wish to generate <note>Referring to the act of human procreation as a physical analogy for divine creation.</note>. However, this is not exactly similar to His act. For He is not poured out by pleasure, nor does He have a helper in His work. Being effective by His own nature, He is always present in the work, acting by His own power, whatever He has ever made. If He should withdraw His influence <note>original: *influxum*, a flowing-in of divine power or spirit.</note> from anything, everything would collapse into death as life failed. Since indeed all things live, and there is one life for all, God is one. 

Again, if all things are living—both those seen in heaven and those lying on the earth—surely one life for all flows from God, and she <note>Life (*vita*) is grammatically feminine in Latin, often personified as a mediating force between God and the world.</note> and He are God. All things are made by one Founder. Life indeed is the union of Mind and soul; death, however, is not the destruction of composite things, but the dissolution of their union.

Therefore, the image of God is Eternity <note>original: *aeum*, referring to the Aeon or the eternal realm beyond time.</note>; of Eternity, the world; of the world, the sun; and of the sun, man. But the common people think that death is a certain change, because the mass of the body is dissolved and life withdraws into the hidden <note>original: *in occultum*, meaning the unseen realm or the source from which it came.</note>. Indeed, oh beloved Mercury, I teach you by this reasoning that the world is changed: because certain of its parts constantly go into the hidden, yet never think that the world itself perishes. These are truly the "passions" <note>original: *passiones*, used here in the philosophical sense of "things undergone" or "experiences," rather than emotions.</note> of the world: namely, revolution and concealment. Revolution is indeed a turning; concealment, however, is renewal. Furthermore, the world, which takes all forms, does not truly receive external or foreign forms; rather, it stirs them up within itself. It will be like the all-formed world; but if it had only a single form, it would be inferior in this world. What, then, shall we say to these things?

-> d iij <-

<summary>The Divine Mind explains that God is the source of all life and is constantly present in His creation. It defines life as the union of mind and soul, while death is merely the dissolution of this union and a return to the "hidden" rather than total destruction.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Mercury, creation, Divine Mind, Aeon, eternity, life and death, dissolution, macrocosm, microcosm, sun, man</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue of the Corpus Hermeticum. On the previous page, the Divine Mind explained that God is the singular source of life, flowing into all things in heaven and on earth. Here, Mercury (Hermes) responds, and the Divine Mind begins a profound explanation of the nature of the "Idea" and the limitless power of the human mind to transcend physical space.</meta>

->MERCURY.<-

I pray that we do not remain in ambiguity. For whatever is ambiguous in divine matters, and not yet known, has a single "idea" <note>The author uses the term "idea" in the Platonic sense: a perfect, non-physical blueprint or "Form" that exists in the mind of God before taking shape in the material world.</note>. Since the specific idea of this thing escapes the sight of our eyes, it is incorporeal <note>meaning it has no physical body or substance</note>; yet it expresses its form through individual physical bodies. Nor should you be overcome with wonder just because there is such a thing as an incorporeal idea. For it is like the "idea" of a spoken word or the small marks <note>original: *apices*, referring to the strokes of a pen or the accents on letters</note> in inscriptions. They seem to stand out prominently, yet by their nature they are clearly weightless and thin. 

But meditate more boldly on what is now said, and on what will be asserted even more truly: just as a human being cannot live without life, so God cannot "live" unless He produces good things. For this is the life of God, this is His action: to move all things and to breathe life into all. 

However, some of the things we have said above require a specific kind of understanding. Consider in this example what I especially wish to signify. All things are in God, but not as if they were placed in a specific physical location. For a "place" is an immobile body, and the things placed within it lack their own motion. God, however, "places" things in a different way: one way in a physical body, and another way in the imagination <note>original: *phantasia*, the faculty of the mind that forms mental images</note>. 

Think of that which contains all individual things; think that there is nothing more capacious than an incorporeal nature, nothing faster, and nothing stronger—it is itself the most capacious, the fastest, and the most powerful of all things. Meditate on this by beginning again from your own self. Command your soul to travel, and it will fly away faster than you can give the command. Command it, I say, to cross over the ocean; it will be there before you have finished the order, and yet it never actually departed from where it currently is. 

Command it again to fly up into heaven: it will need no wings. Nothing will obstruct its course—not the burning heat of the sun, nor the vastness of the ether <note>The "ether" was considered the fifth element, the pure, fiery substance that filled the space of the heavens above the clouds.</note>, nor the rotation of the heavens, nor the bodies of the other stars. Instead, penetrating all things, it may transcend even to the highest celestial body. Furthermore, if you wish to pass through all the spheres of the heavens themselves and investigate what lies above them, that also is possible...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue within the Corpus Hermeticum. Hermes (Mercury) is receiving a revelation from the Divine Mind (often called Poimandres or Nous) regarding the relationship between the physical world and the spiritual "Ideas" that govern it. The text focuses on the omnipresence of God and the nature of the incorporeal.</meta>

->MERCURY.<-

I pray that we do not remain in ambiguity. For whatever is ambiguous in divine matters, and not yet known, has a single "idea" <note>The author uses the term "idea" in the Platonic sense: a perfect, non-physical blueprint or "Form" that exists in the mind of God before taking shape in the material world.</note>. Since the specific idea of this thing escapes the sight of our eyes, it is incorporeal <note>meaning it has no physical body or substance</note>; yet it expresses its form through individual physical bodies. Nor should you be overcome with wonder just because there is such a thing as an incorporeal idea. For it is like the "idea" of a spoken word or the small marks <note>original: *apices*, referring to the strokes of a pen or the accents on letters</note> in inscriptions. They seem to stand out prominently, yet by their nature they are clearly weightless and thin.

But meditate more boldly on what is now said, and on what will be asserted even more truly: just as a human being cannot live without life, so God cannot "live" unless He produces good things. For this is the life of God, this is His action: to move all things and to breathe life into all.

However, some of the things we have said above require a specific kind of understanding. Consider in this example what I especially wish to signify. All things are in God, but not as if they were placed in a specific physical location. For a "place" is an immobile body, and the things placed within it lack their own motion. God, however, "places" things in a different way: one way in a physical body, and another way in the mind.

<summary>Mercury explains that divine "Ideas" are incorporeal blueprints that give shape to the material world, and that God’s very existence is defined by His constant action of creating and moving all things.</summary>
<keywords>Hermeticism, Platonic Ideas, incorporeal, divine action, nature of God, Corpus Hermeticum, Mercury</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page concludes the tenth book of the Hermetic corpus, where the Divine Mind finishes its instruction to Hermes Trismegistus. It then transitions into a summary and introduction to the eleventh book, focusing on the hierarchy of existence from God down to life and death, and concludes with the heading for the twelfth book.</meta>

...nature: because it does not bear within itself some image of divinity.

->TRISMEGISTUS.<-

Is God not invisible?

->THE MIND.<-

Speak more piously, Trismegistus. For who is more manifest than He? Indeed, He fashioned all things for this very reason: so that you might perceive Him through each individual thing. This is the goodness of God, and this is His power: that He shines through all things. Nothing is invisible, even among things that have no body. The mind itself is seen through the act of understanding <note>The author uses "intellectione," referring to the internal "sight" of the soul</note>, whereas God is perceived through His works. Let these matters be unfolded for you thus far, Trismegistus; all other things you shall now investigate for yourself, so that you will not be deceived by a false image of the truth.

### The Eleventh Book: Concerning the Mind

This eleventh discourse, full of divine speech, declares that eternity depends on God, the world on eternity, time on the world, and change on time; furthermore, it shows that both life and death hang upon this chain of change. It demonstrates most gracefully that no power—whether divine, human, or belonging to the beings of the heavens or the underworld—is comparable to the insuperable power of God. He is shown to be the only One through both reason and analogies. Since He cannot be idle, He is always making all things. 

You may rightly understand this as referring to the eternal generation of the Word <note>The "Word" (Logos) here refers to the creative power of God, a concept shared by both Hermetic philosophers and early Christians</note>. For the text says it was necessary for the divine work to be present so that those things might exist which are currently being made, those which were made in the past, and those which will be made in the future. That work is life; it is the beautiful; it is the good; it is, finally, God and the life of all things. 

But what is this, if not what the trumpet of the Evangelist sang more loftily when he said: *In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. This was in the beginning with God; all things were made through Him, and without Him nothing was made.* <note>original: "In principio erat verbum..." The author is explicitly quoting John 1:1-3 to show the harmony between Hermetic wisdom and the Bible.</note> That which was made in Him was life. Therefore, the life of all things flows from God and is God Himself. 

From this, it follows that true death is not the destruction of things, as the common people believe. Rather, "death" is merely the name for when the life of living things, withdrawing itself into a hidden place, leaves the physical body. Indeed, the author uses a wonderful proportion to try to grasp Him who escapes all proportion; who, though understood, remains incomprehensible; and who, though unknown to all, makes Himself knowable in all things. 

Therefore, all you who are pious, attend here now with your whole being, and incline your own minds to the discourses of the Mind, so that you may at last know Him who fashioned all things for this reason: so that you might perceive Him through each individual thing.

Thus far the Divine Mind.

### ¶ XII. Of Mercury to Tat, concerning common things.

<meta>This page continues the Hermetic dialogues, transitioning into a discourse from Hermes Trismegistus to his son, Tat. It explores the nature of the Divine Mind (Nous) and its relationship to the human soul, specifically how it acts as a corrective force against the base impulses of the body.</meta>

I	26 29

Indeed, O Tat, the Mind <note>The translator uses "Mens" to represent the Greek "Nous"—not merely human intellect, but a divine spark of intuitive understanding</note> is born from the very essence of God—if indeed there is any essence of God. Whatever this essence may be, it alone truly understands itself. The Mind, therefore, is in no way divided from the essence of God, but is rather connected to it in the same way as light is to the body of the sun. This Mind is God in humans; and for that reason, some among the number of men are gods, and their humanity is closest to divinity. For indeed, the Good Spirit <note>original: "bonus demon," referring to the Agathos Daimon, a primary teacher in the Hermetic tradition</note> calls the gods "immortal humans," and humans "mortal gods."

In irrational animals, however, that Mind is simply their nature. For wherever there is a soul, there also is Mind; just as wherever there is life, there also is a soul in living beings. Without the discourse of reason, the soul is life empty of Mind. Indeed, the Mind is the helper of human souls, calling them back toward their own proper good. In animals lacking reason, Mind works in harmony with the nature of each. In the souls of humans, however, it sometimes resists and fights back. For since the soul is confined within the body, it is continuously corrupted by pleasure and pain. From the mixture of the body, pleasure and pain spring forth like streams, in which the saturated soul is drowned.

Therefore, over whichever souls the Mind presides, it illuminates them with its own brilliance, resisting their habits and evils. Just as a learned physician subjects the body of a sick person to pain by burning and cutting it for the sake of recovering health, in the same way, the Mind afflicts the pleasure-seeking soul so that it may pull out the roots of pleasure. For from this pleasure comes every disease of the soul; the most serious disease of the soul is impiety. False opinion <note>original: "opinio," referring to "doxa" or belief without true knowledge</note> does not entice toward any good at all, but rather toward evil. But that Mind, by resisting, provides for the good of the soul, just as the physician provides for the health of the body. As for those human souls that do not have the Mind as a strong governor, they suffer the same things as the souls of brute beasts. It dismisses...

<summary>This page defines the Mind (Mens/Nous) as an extension of God's essence that dwells within humans, elevating them to the status of "mortal gods." It describes the Mind's role as a spiritual "physician" that must sometimes cause the soul pain to purge it of the corrupting influences of bodily pleasure and impiety.</summary>
<keywords>Mind, Nous, Essence of God, Agathos Daimon, immortality, soul, pleasure and pain, spiritual physician, impiety, Tat, Hermes Trismegistus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous section where Hermes Trismegistus compared the Divine Mind to a physician treating the soul's illness. The text now moves into the problem of free will versus predestination (Fate).</meta>

MER

...the Mind subdues them from the rule of desires; toward the fulfillment of which they are carried by a certain burning impulse, and in the manner of wild beasts, they are immoderately angry and full of lust. And what is worse, they set no end to their lust, nor do they find a limit to their evils and passions. To these, God has set forth the Law like a lictor <note>In ancient Rome, a lictor was an officer who carried the fasces (a bundle of rods and an axe) and executed the sentences of magistrates. Here, the Law acts as a punisher or enforcer of divine justice.</note> to serve as a judge.

TAT. Here, O father, that discussion about Fate (which we left unfinished in the earlier parts) comes back to life. For if it is decreed by Fate that some are adulterers and some perhaps sacrilegious, why is anyone punished for it, since they have committed the crime compelled by the necessity of Fate?

TRISMEGISTUS. All things are the works of Fate, my son. Without it, nothing exists among bodily things. Neither can anything good happen without it, nor anything evil. It is decreed that he who has committed something shameful shall suffer. He commits the act for this very reason: so that he may suffer whatever he suffers as a result of having committed it. Moreover, concerning the punishments of Fate and of crimes, we have spoken in other places. But our present discourse is about the Mind: what the Mind is capable of, and how its instinct differs in humans and in brute animals. And again, how in beasts the Mind itself is not beneficial, but in humans it restrains the impulse of lust and extinguishes the fire of anger. From which it follows that among men, some exist as irrational and some as rational.

Indeed, all humans are subject to Fate, to birth, and to transformation <note>original: "generationi / transmutationi." In Hermetic thought, these are the mechanical processes of the physical universe governed by the stars.</note>; for these two—birth and transformation—are the beginning and the end of Fate. And all humans truly suffer that which Fate has ordained. But those rational ones, whom we said the Mind assists as a governor, do not suffer in the same way as the others. Rather, being far from wickedness and not being evil, they suffer very little evil.

TAT. Why do you say these things again, O father?

TRISMEGISTUS. Is the adulterer not evil? Is the murderer not evil? Yet the man who is not an adulterer may still suffer, but in the same manner as an adulterer... <note>The text likely continues to explain that while a good person may face physical hardships (Fate), they do not suffer the spiritual corruption or "evil" that the wicked do.</note>

<summary>Hermes explains that while Fate governs all physical events and births, the Divine Mind allows rational humans to master their impulses and remain spiritually untainted by the "evil" of their physical circumstances.</summary>
<keywords>Fate, Divine Mind, free will, Law, justice, rationality, birth, transformation, adultery, punishment</keywords>

I
...a murderer; but in what way is he a murderer? It is impossible to escape the quality of change, just as it is impossible to escape the effect of birth. However, it is possible for one who possesses Mind to avoid wickedness. For this reason, my son, I have always listened to the Good Daemon <note>original: *bonum demonem*. In Hermetic tradition, the "Good Daemon" or Agathos Daimon is a figure of supreme wisdom, often described as the first teacher of Hermes or a personification of Divine Intellect.</note> dictating to me. If he had committed his records to writing, he would have bestowed wonderful benefits upon the human race every day. He alone, my son—as if he were a god born for my sake—beholding all things, has poured forth divine oracles upon us. Indeed, I once heard him preaching in this way: "All things are one, especially intelligible <note>The "intelligible" world refers to reality that is understood through the intellect and spirit, rather than through the physical senses.</note> things. We live as bodies by power, by action, and by eternity." His Mind is good, just as his soul is good. Since this is the case, nothing among the intelligible is distant from another intelligible thing. 

Moreover, the Mind—the prince of all things and the very soul of God—is able to bring about whatever it wills. These were his words. You, therefore, consider these matters; and as for this discourse on Fate which I am about to introduce, attend to it with your mind and with ears alert. If you diligently avoid contentious quibbles, you will find without a doubt that Mind, being the soul of God, dominates all things—both the Law and all other things in the universe. Nor is anything pertaining to Fate impossible for the Mind to overcome. Therefore, the human soul is superior to Fate, yet it does not neglect the things that are subject to Fate. These are the excellent oracles of the Good Daemon. 

TAT: Truly, O father, you have treated these matters in a divine and fitting manner. But I beg you to explain one thing further. You said that in brute animals, Mind operates according to the way of nature, cooperating with their instincts <note>original: *affectibus*, referring to the innate drives or dispositions of animals.</note>. However, the instincts of irrational creatures (as I believe) are passions <term>Passions: In classical philosophy, "passions" (passiones) are not just strong emotions, but things that "happen" to a person or animal—external influences or internal drives that one "suffers" or undergoes without active choice.</term>. Therefore, is Mind itself a kind of passion? For it seems to be shaped by those passions. 

TRISMEGISTUS: Well done, my good son! You ask a noble question, and it is right that I answer you. All incorporeal things that exist within bodies are subject to being acted upon <note>original: *patibilia*. This suggests they are "passive" or capable of receiving impressions.</note>; indeed, they are strictly speaking "passions." Every motion of the body...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius) and his son Tat. The previous page ended with a discussion on the Mind being the soul of God and its relation to Fate. This page transitions into the nature of movement, "passion" (as a philosophical concept of being acted upon), and the unique gifts of speech and Mind given to humanity.</meta>

->MER.<-
[cor]poreal <note>The Latin text begins with the suffix *-poreus*, completing the word *corporeus* (corporeal) from the previous page.</note>: the incorporeal is also moved by the Mind. Moreover, movement is a passion <note>In this philosophical context, "passion" (Latin: *passio*) does not mean emotion, but the state of being acted upon or "undergoing" an effect from an outside force.</note>. Therefore, both that which is moved and that which moves undergo passion: the latter as the master, the former as the subject. But when it is separated from the body, it is liberated from passions. Indeed, nothing is entirely impassible <note>meaning "unable to be affected"</note>, my son; for all things are subject to passion.

But passion and that which is "passible" differ in this especially: that one acts, and the other undergoes. Bodies, however, also act according to themselves; for they are either immobile or they are moved. Whichever of these it may be, it is a passion. Incorporeal things, on the other hand, are always active, and for that reason, they are passible <note>Hermes suggests here that because they are always "operating" or "acting," they exist within the system of cause and effect.</note>. Do not let these names disturb you. For operation and passion are the same thing; nevertheless, it does no harm to use a more honorable term.

**TAT.** You have given a clear account of these things, O father.

**TRISME.** Consider this further, my son: that God himself has bestowed these two things upon man alone out of all living creatures—namely, speech <note>original: *sermonem*. This refers to *Logos*, which encompasses both spoken language and the rational thought behind it.</note> and the Mind. These are considered to be of the same value as immortality. Whoever uses these for what is proper differs in no way from the immortals. Furthermore, once released from the shackles of the body, he shall be led by both into the choir of the blessed ones and the gods.

**TAT.** Do not other living creatures besides man use speech?

**TRISME.** No, my son, but rather voice <note>original: *voce*. Hermes distinguishes between "voice" (animal sounds/biological noise) and "speech" (rational, structured communication/Logos).</note>. There is a great difference between voice and speech. Speech is common to all men; but voice is specific to each individual man, and likewise specific to each species of animal.

**TAT.** O father, different types of men use different languages <note>original: *sermonibus*.</note>.

**TRISME.** They are diverse, my son. Yet speech is one, which, translated here and there through interpretation, is found to be one and the same Word <note>original: *verbum*. Here Hermes aligns the concept of the *Logos* across different cultures.</note>. This Word is the same among the Egyptians, the Persians, and the Greeks. But you seem, my son, to be ignorant of the power and the greatness of the Word.

<summary>Hermes explains the philosophical relationship between movement and "passion," and identifies speech (Logos) and Mind as the two divine gifts that grant humans immortality and distinguish them from animals.</summary>
<keywords>Logos, Mind, passion, immortality, speech vs voice, Agathos Daimon, Hermeticism, universal language</keywords>

...corporeal <note>The text begins with the completion of the word "corporeal," continuing from the previous page's discussion on physical versus spiritual substances.</note>: the incorporeal is also moved by the Mind. Moreover, movement is a **passion** <note>In this philosophical context, "passion" (from the Latin *passio*) does not mean an emotion like love or anger. Instead, it refers to the state of being acted upon or "undergoing" a change from an external force.</note>. Therefore, both that which is moved and that which moves undergo passion: the latter as the master, the former as the subject. But when it is separated from the body, it is liberated from passions. Indeed, nothing is entirely *impassible* <note>meaning "unable to be affected by anything else"</note>, my son; for all things are subject to passion.

But passion and that which is "passible" differ in this especially: that one acts, and the other undergoes. Bodies, however, also act according to themselves; for they are either immobile or they are moved. Whichever of these it may be, it is a passion. Incorporeal things, on the other hand, are always active, and for that reason, they are passible <note>Hermes is explaining a paradox: because the soul and mind are constantly "operating" or "acting," they are always in a state of "undergoing" their own activity.</note>. Do not let these names disturb you. For operation and passion are the same thing; nevertheless, it does no harm to use a more honorable term.

**TAT.** You have given a clear account of these things, O father.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Consider this further, my son: that God himself has bestowed these two things upon man alone out of all living creatures—namely, **speech** <note>original: *sermonem*. In this tradition, this refers to *Logos*, which represents both the spoken word and the divine reason behind it.</note> and **Mind**. These gifts are considered equal to immortality. If a man uses these two gifts as he should, he will differ in nothing from the immortals; and when he leaves the body, these two will be his guides, leading him to the choir of the gods and the souls that have attained bliss.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous section where Hermes Trismegistus explained the nature of "passion" and movement. Here, the focus shifts to the nature of death and the unique spiritual status of humanity within the living world.</meta>

->MERCURY<-

**TAT.** O father, do not the living beings that are in the world—which are parts of the world—die? 

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Speak more correctly, my son. For you err in the term you use. Nothing in the world dies; rather, composite bodies <note>In Hermetic philosophy, "composite bodies" are physical things made of a mixture of elements. When they "die," the mixture simply pulls apart.</note> are dissolved. This dissolution is not death, but a certain resolution of the mixture. The union is dissolved, not so that those things which exist might perish, but so that old things might become young again. 

**TAT.** Since this is a certain operation of life, is it not also a kind of motion? And therefore, is anything in the world immobile? 

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Nothing, my son. 

**TAT.** Does the earth not seem immobile to you? 

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Not at all; though shaken by many movements, it alone is nevertheless stable in a certain way. Would it not be ridiculous for the very nurse of all things—conceiving and bringing forth every single thing—to lack motion? For it is impossible for anything to give birth without motion. It is no less ridiculous to call that body sterile; for the name "immobile" signifies nothing other than something sterile. 

Consider this whole process of generation <note>original: *generatio*, referring to the act of bringing things into being or being born.</note>, my son, which is in the world: it moves either by growing or by decreasing. What moves, moreover, lives. Yet it is not necessary for all living things to be the same. At the same time, the entire world, as it exists, is indeed immobile as a whole, but its parts are movable on all sides; yet nothing is subject to corruption <note>In this context, "corruption" refers to the total destruction of a thing's essence, rather than just decay.</note>, though certain false names disturb men. 

For generation is not the creation of life, but the unfolding <note>original: *explicatio*, literally an "unrolling" or "unfolding" of something already there but hidden.</note> of a life that was hidden; nor is change death, but rather a concealment. Since things are so, all things are immortal. The matter of life is spirit; the mind is the matter of the soul. Every living thing flows forth from the Mind. Therefore, everything living through the Mind remains immortal. 

But man is the most immortal of all, for he is the one who grasps God and is conformed to the divine essence; for to him alone among all kinds of living things does God himself draw near. By night He comes through dreams, and more frequently by day through portents <note>Signs or omens that were believed to indicate divine will or future events.</note>, through all of which He foretells the future to him. By birds...

<summary>Hermes explains to Tat that death is merely the dissolution of parts and that the Earth is a living, moving mother. He concludes by highlighting humanity's unique, immortal connection to God through the Mind and divine signs.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, immortality, dissolution vs death, generation, movement of the Earth, Mind (Mens), divine portents, human essence</keywords>

...through the intestines <note>This refers to the ancient practice of *haruspicy*—divining the future by inspecting the internal organs of sacrificed animals.</note>; through the spirit; and through the Sibyl <note>A Sibyl was a prophetess in the ancient world who delivered oracles under divine inspiration.</note>. Because of these things, man is truly said to know the things that are, the things that were, and the things that are soon to be brought to pass. 

But first of all, my son, you should notice that each genus of living beings inhabits its own province of the world: the wet creatures inhabit the water; the terrestrial, the earth; and the flying creatures, the air. Man, however, makes use of all of these: earth, water, air, and fire. He gazes up at heaven and reaches it with his very sense. Truly, God is around all things at once and through all things; for He is the act and the power of all things. 

For it is a difficult thing, O son, to perceive God. Whenever you wish to look upon Him, observe the order of the world and the beauty of that order. Notice the necessity of those things perceived by the senses, and the providence over all things: both those things made in the past and those things which are made daily. Notice the matter full of life, and such a great God walking with all good and beautiful gods, demons <note>The original term *daemonibus* refers to "daemons"—guiding spirits or intermediary beings between gods and humans—rather than the modern concept of "demons."</note>, and men. 

**TAT.** But father, these are surely certain operations <note>original: *operationes*, referring to the active functions or workings of a higher power.</note>.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Ah, my son, from where do these things proceed if not from God himself? Are you unaware that just as heaven, water, earth, and air are parts of the world, in the same way life, immortality, necessity, providence, nature, soul, and mind are the members of God? And the participation in all of these is the "Good" itself. Nor can anything else ever be, nor is it ever made, where God himself is not present.

**TAT.** Is He in matter then, father?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Matter, my son, is separate from God, so that a place proper to it may be assigned. What else do you think matter is but this raw and unordered mass <note>original: *rudem indigestaque congeriem*, a classic Latin phrase (notably used by Ovid) to describe the state of Chaos before the world was ordered.</note>? Do not believe it could exist unless it were formed. But if it is formed, it is surely formed by someone; for we have said that the operations of forming are themselves parts of God. By whom, therefore, is life given to individual living things? By whom do those who enjoy the gift of immortality become immortal? By whom...

<summary>Hermes explains that God is the underlying "act and power" of the entire universe, manifesting through the four elements and through abstract qualities like providence and mind. He clarifies that matter is a "raw mass" that requires God's formative "operation" to exist as an ordered world.</summary>
<keywords>divination, four elements, providence, daemons, nature of God, matter, chaos, immortality, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page concludes the eleventh book of the Hermetic corpus, where Hermes explains that God is the source of all activity in both matter and spirit. It then transitions into the introduction and the beginning of the twelfth book, which focuses on the "Inferior Mind" and the distinction between human reason and animal instinct.</meta>

->MER.<-

[...those things] that are changeable—whether you call them matter or body—remember that they are the acts of God. And the act of matter is the **reason** <note>original: *rationem*. In this context, it refers to the underlying logic, proportion, or governing principle of a thing.</note> of matter; the bodily act is the reason of bodies; the act of essence is the reason of essence. And this is the All; nor is there anything in the whole universe which is not God Himself. Therefore, neither magnitude, nor place, nor quality, nor shape, nor even time exists around God, for He is the All. The All, however, is around all things and through all things. Cherish and adore this word, O son. Moreover, the worship of God is one: **not to be evil.**

### Chapter Twelve: Concerning the inferior mind (which is a certain image or footprint of the primary mind)

This chapter, following after the precise definition of the primary mind, associates that mind with individual souls. But in irrational beings, the mind is simply nature; in men, however, it is **Reason** <note>original: *ratio*. The text distinguishes between the basic animating "mind" of animals and the higher "reason" of humans.</note>. By the benefit of this reason, whoever among men is destitute of it is carried away in the manner of wild beasts by a certain flaming impulse toward the fulfillment of desires. They set no limit to their lust, nor do they find an end to their evils and passions. Those who suffer by fate and divine decree pay the deserved penalties for their ignorance.

The good, on the other hand—over whom the Mind presides as the image of the Good and the Beautiful—receive the good things which Fate has ordained for them. You will by no means escape Fate; for Fate is indeed the divine law. Nevertheless, you can escape the evils and the punishments that follow wickedness.

Mercury, full of oracular power, discusses many things. He has often heard the oracle pouring forth wisdom. He commends two divine gifts in men: **Mind** and **Speech**. He opens the oracles of the **Good Spirit** <note>original: *boni demonis*. In the original Greek, this is the *Agathos Daimon*, a benevolent divine intellect or "teacher" spirit, not a "demon" in the modern malevolent sense.</note>—or, if you prefer, the celestial spirit—to those divinely inspired, concerning God, the Word, the world, and matter:

1. That nothing in the world is devoid of life. 
2. That nothing should be called mortal (but rather should be understood as something **dissoluble** <note>original: *dissolubile*. Hermetic philosophy teaches that nothing truly "dies" or ceases to exist; rather, the components of a body simply "dissolve" or break apart to be reused in new forms.</note>). 
3. That nothing is immobile.

And he explains that God joins with man alone among dissoluble beings in various ways, and how man can rise from sensible things to the perception of God. These things are in the twelfth chapter.

The body of the sensory nature differs greatly from essential birth <note>original: *essentiali generatione*. He exhorted me to honorable duties, from which all the powers that are within me now sing in harmony.

TAT: O father, I would wish to hear and understand these things.

TRISMEGISTUS: Be still, my son; be still, I say. You shall now hear the harmonic song, the hymn of rebirth <note>original: *regenerationis*. This is a sacred song celebrating the soul's transformation and its return to God.</note>, which I would never so easily make public were I not considering what would be of benefit to you. This is not taught; rather, it is hidden in silence. Look at me, good son; observe each thing diligently. For it befits you also, when praying under the open sky at sunset, to turn your face toward the south; and at sunrise, to turn toward the east. Let the entire nature of the world hear this hymn.

Hear, O Earth! Hear, O whirlwinds of rain! O forests, be silent! I am about to sing of the Creator of all, the Whole and the One. Hear, O Heavens! Be still, O winds! Let the circle of the immortal God hear this prayer. I sing now of the Creator of all, the distributor of lands, the one who balances the heavens, commanding the sweet water to flow from the ocean everywhere for the nourishment of men, and commanding the fire to shine from above for the actions of men and gods. Let us all give thanks to Him with one voice: we who have passed through the heavens and the nature of things created. This is the eye of the mind; He will gladly receive this blessing from the powers. O my powers, praise Him, the One and the All! Sing in harmony with His pleasure!

is about the secret mystery of regeneration, before which no one was saved. The author of this regeneration is the **Son of God**, man, by the will of the one God. It is a mystery that is not taught, but is venerated by the eyes in silence, and in silence by deeds.

Having been entirely turned toward God the Parent and the Son, the author of regeneration, you have obtained this fruit from this thirteenth treatise—or at least were moved by the goodness of your mind toward such pious contemplation.

<margin>q</margin> ->### The Epilogue of Mercurius to Aesculapius. XIV.<-

Since my son <term>Tat</term> <note>original: *Tatius*. Hermes' son and primary disciple in the Hermetic dialogues.</note> (in your absence) wished to learn the nature of all things, and since he himself did not want me to hesitate—inasmuch as he is younger and a recent newcomer to the knowledge of everything—I was forced to say many things, so that the insight might be easy and ready for him. I judged it to be worthwhile that, choosing the more important points from the many, I might now repeat the preceding discussion for you in summary. For it is permitted to run through certain mysteries with you, as one skilled in the nature of things. All things that are perceived by the senses have been made, and are made daily. Things born are not made by themselves, but by another. Many things are born, each manifest to the sense, and differing and not altogether similar. Whatever things are born proceed from...

<meta>This page continues the transition from the end of the previous discourse on spiritual rebirth to a new letter addressed to Aesculapius. ->MERCURY<-

...by another. Therefore, there is someone who is the **Maker** <note>original: *factor*. In Hermetic philosophy, the "Maker" is the divine architect who brings order to the universe.</note> of these things; and he is **unbegotten** <note>original: *ingenitus*. This term describes a being that was not created by a prior cause, but exists eternally of itself.</note>, so that he is more ancient than those things which are created. For we have said that **created things** <note>original: *genita*. These are temporal beings or objects that have a beginning in time, unlike the eternal Creator.</note> flow from another. But nothing except the unbegotten can be more ancient than the created things.

The Maker, indeed, is more powerful and unique, and he alone is truly wise over all things, since absolutely nothing precedes him. He also holds dominion over multitude, magnitude, action, continuity, and the differences of all created things. Furthermore, created things are visible, while he remains invisible. It is for this very reason that he acts: so that he may become visible. Therefore, he is always acting.

It is worthy to understand; through understanding, to marvel; and in marvelling, to call oneself **blessed**, recognizing the true Father. For what is sweeter than a true parent? Who is this one? How shall we find him? Is it fitting to attribute to him the name of **God** alone, or of **Maker**, or of **Father**? Or perhaps all three names together? Therefore, let him be named **God** because of his power, and **Maker** because...

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus explains to Aesculapius that God is the eternal, uncreated "Maker" who manifests himself through the visible world. He argues that the Creator is more ancient than any created thing and invites the seeker to find joy in recognizing God as their true Father.</summary>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument addressed to Aesculapius regarding the relationship between the Maker and the thing made. It follows the previous page's discussion on why God is called Father and Maker, moving now into a defense of God’s constant creative activity and an explanation for the existence of "evil" as a byproduct of material generation.</meta>

I                                                                 27 37

...in existing equally and in acting. Nothing, however, that is **begotten** <note>original: *genitum*. This refers to anything that has been created or brought into being, emphasizing its status as a product of a higher cause.</note> is begotten of itself. Therefore, it is not made apart from the one who makes it. He who takes away one of these—the maker or the thing made—loses the other as well.

For the one, by its own nature, always regards the other nature. If, therefore, two things are granted—the **producer** and the **produced**—they are indeed united to each other, yet in such a way that one precedes and the other follows. The preceding is surely God the producer; the following is that which is produced.

Whatever that may be, let no one distrust what we have said, being terrified by the variety of things, as if the construction of such diverse things were either difficult or unworthy of the **divine majesty**. For God has a unique glory: the constitution of all things, and that is, as it were, the **body of God the maker** <note>original: *corpus dei effector*. A central Hermetic idea suggesting that the entire physical universe is the visible manifestation or "body" of the Creator’s activity.</note>. 

From the maker himself, nothing is evil and nothing is shameful. For these **passions** <note>original: *passiones*. In this philosophical context, "passions" refers to accidental defects or "suffering" inherent in matter, rather than human emotions.</note> are things that follow upon created works, just as rust follows brass, or filth follows living bodies. But neither did the blacksmith introduce the rust, nor did the parent of the living body introduce the mud and dross. In the same way, God did not make evil.

But the persistence of **generation** <note>original: *generationis*. The continuous cycle of birth, growth, and decay in the physical world.</note> forces evil to creep in. For that reason, God established change in things as a kind of purification of generation itself.

Furthermore, it is permitted for a painter to represent the sky, the earth, the sea, gods, humans, beasts, trees, and things lacking life; shall the faculty for doing such things be lacking to the one God? O madman, O blind one, inexperienced in all divine knowledge! 

Nothing is more ridiculous, O **Aesculapius** <note>The legendary student of Hermes Trismegistus, often identified with the Greek god of medicine.</note>, than what happens to such a man: for while he claims to venerate God by "freeing" Him from the labor and care of creating, he is entirely ignorant of God himself. And what is worse, he ascribes to Him the passions of mortals: envy, pride, ignorance, and weakness. For unless He makes all things, He is either proud or rather weak; both of which are wicked to suggest. For God has a unique and proper nature...

<summary>Hermes explains to Aesculapius that God is constantly creating and that "evil" is not a divine creation but an accidental byproduct of the material world, much like rust on metal. He rebukes those who think God is too "exalted" to bother with creation, arguing that God's very nature is to produce all things.</summary>
<keywords>Maker and Made, theodicy, origin of evil, Aesculapius, divine majesty, body of God, generation and decay, Hermetic philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Hermes (Mercurius) to Aesculapius. It transitions from the definition of the Maker to the character of God as the "Good," using a pastoral analogy to explain how divine influence is distributed across different levels of reality (the heavens and the earth).</meta>

HERMES.

...possesses it: and that itself is good. But he who is good is neither proud nor powerless. For the Good itself is God. Indeed, the Good is the very **power** <term>original: *vis*. Here meaning the active, driving force or efficacy behind all actions.</term> of all things that are to be done. Whatever is **begotten** <note>original: *genitum*. This refers to anything that has been brought into being or generated, emphasizing that it has a source.</note> is begotten by God: that is, by the Good and by Him who is all-powerful. 

See how He indeed acts, and how those things are also made. If you wish to understand this, you may contemplate a great, most beautiful, and very similar analogy. 

I pray you, observe the farmer scattering seeds into the womb of the earth: here wheat, there barley, and elsewhere seeds of other kinds. Observe the same man **planting anew** <note>original: *repastinantem*. A technical agricultural term for digging or trenching the ground again to prepare it for vines or trees.</note> and pruning vines, apple trees, and fig trees. 

In the same way, God Himself sows **immortality** into the heavens, but **change** <note>original: *mutationem*. In this philosophy, "change" is the terrestrial version of death; things on earth do not perish entirely but merely shift their form or state.</note> into the earth; and finally, life and motion into the whole world. 

These things, however, are not many: but

<summary>Hermes defines God as the "Good" and the source of all creative power, using the analogy of a farmer sowing different crops to explain how God distributes immortality to the heavens and change to the earth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory framing of the *Asclepius*, a core text of the Hermetic tradition. Having previously established God as the source of "the Good" and the farmer of the universe, the text now shifts to the narrative setting of the dialogue: a private, sacred initiation involving Hermes and three select students—Asclepius, Hammon, and Tat.</meta>

II                                                                                                   28 38

¶ The dialogue of **Mercurius Trismegistus**, translated by the Platonic philosopher **Lucius Apuleius of Madaura**. <note>Apuleius was a 2nd-century Roman writer best known for *The Golden Ass*. Historically, he was credited with this Latin translation of the Greek *Asclepius*.</note>

                                                                                                    I
<margin>The four men: Asclepius, Hammon, Mercurius; the fourth, from the second chapter, is Tat.</margin>This **Asclepius** <note>original: *Sclepius*. The initial 'A' was likely a decorative dropped-cap omitted in the transcription.</note> is like the sun to me. God has brought you to us, O Asclepius, so that you might take part in a divine discourse—one which, by right, seems more divine in its religious piety than all those we have previously made, or those inspired in us by the divine power. If you perceive this with an understanding eye, you will be filled in your whole mind with all good things—if, indeed, there are many "goods," and not just one in which all things are contained. For it is recognized that one is consistent with the other: that all things are of the One, or that the One is all things; for the one is so connected to the other that it is impossible to separate them. But you will learn this through diligent attention in the discourse to follow. 

Now you, O Asclepius, go forward a little and call the one who should be present. 

When he had entered, Asclepius suggested that **Hammon** should also be present. Trismegistus said: 

"No ill-will keeps Hammon from us. Indeed, we remember having written many things in his name, just as we have written many things for our most beloved and dear son [Tat]—much concerning physics and very many **exoteric** <note>original: *exotica*. This likely refers to "outer" or "public" teachings as opposed to the "inner" or secret ones being shared here.</note> matters. However, I shall dedicate this treatise to your name. But call no one else besides Hammon, lest the most holy discourse on so great a matter be profaned by the arrival and presence of many interveners. For it is the mark of an irreligious mind to publish to the knowledge of the many a treatise filled with the total majesty of the divinity."

<margin>*Hermu* is the Greek genitive [form of the name Hermes].</margin>When Hammon had also entered the **sanctuary** <term>original: *adytum*. The innermost, most sacred chamber of a temple, where only the initiated could enter.</term>, and that holy place was filled with the piety of the four men and the divine presence of God, with everyone's soul and mind hanging in appropriate and venerable silence upon the words from the mouth of Hermes, **divine Love** <note>original: *Cupido*. While usually a name for a god, here it represents the spiritual longing or inspiration that drives the teacher to speak.</note> began to speak thus: 

"TRISMEGISTUS: O Asclepius, every human soul is immortal..."

<summary>Hermes (Mercurius) gathers a small circle of three disciples—Asclepius, Hammon, and Tat—into a sacred sanctuary for a private revelation, emphasizing that such deep divine truths must be kept from the common public to preserve their sanctity.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, Hammon, Tat, sacred silence, initiation, monism, divinity, immortality of the soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue in the inner sanctum. Hermes (Mercurius) has just invited his students to listen closely to a "divine discourse" regarding the unity of the universe. The previous page ended with Asclepius calling the third student, Hammon, into the room to complete the sacred circle of four.</meta>

**MERCURIUS.**
...is the soul: but not all souls are uniform; rather, some are in one way, others in another, or at different times.

**ASCLEPIUS.**
For is not every soul of one quality, O Trismegistus? <note>original: *o trismegiste*. The name "Trismegistus" means "Thrice-Greatest," a title for Hermes reflecting his mastery over the three parts of the wisdom of the whole universe: alchemy, astrology, and theurgy.</note>

**TRISMEGISTUS.**
O Asclepius, how quickly you have learned the true substance of reason! For I did not say this—that all things are one and the One is all things—as if all things were already in the Creator before he created all things. Nor is he called "all things" without cause, for all things are his members. Therefore, throughout this entire discussion, take care to remember him who is the "One-All," or the Creator of all things himself. 

All things descend from heaven—into the earth, the water, and the air. Only fire, which is carried upwards, is life-giving; that which moves downwards is its servant. Indeed, whatever descends from on high is procreative; whatever flows upwards is nourishing. The earth alone, standing firm in itself, is the receiver of all things and the restorer of every kind it receives. 

Therefore, within this whole—which is "of all" or "all things," as you recall—the soul and the world are set in motion, held together by nature. They are so varied by a balanced equality of multiform images that infinite species of qualities are known to exist across intervals. Yet they are joined together so that the whole appears as one, and all things appear to come from the One. 

Thus, the whole from which the world is formed consists of four elements: fire, water, earth, and air. There is one world, one soul, and one God. Now, be present with me with all the strength of your mind and all the cleverness you possess. For the reason of divinity—which must be known through the divine focus of the senses—is most like a torrential river, rushing down from the heights with headlong speed. Consequently, it surpasses the focus not only of those listening but even of those discussing it, by its swift velocity.

<gloss>In the first part, he teaches that all things belong to the One, and all things are the One. "Of the One" means that from him all things come. "The One" means that all things created are one in their cause. He teaches that every human soul is immortal, but they are of different [qualities].</gloss>

<summary>Hermes explains the philosophical unity of the universe, arguing that while all things are diverse, they are ultimately "members" of the One Creator. He describes the movement of the four elements and warns that divine wisdom is as swift and difficult to grasp as a rushing torrent.</summary>
<keywords>Hermeticism, Oneness, Four Elements, immortality of the soul, Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, divinity, cosmology</keywords>

                                  II                                  29  39
...perhaps. That all things descend from heaven; that which descends provides generation. That which ascends and tends upward alone provides life. This very One, to whom all things belong and who is all things, moves the world and all the forms of the world. Of these, the world was formed: fire, air, water, and earth. And just as all bodies constitute one body of one world, so too he <note>Referring to the Creator/Demiurge</note> wills all the forms of things to constitute one all-encompassing form of the one world, which he calls the <term>Soul of the World</term> <note>original: *animam mundi*; the vital force that animates the physical universe</note>. For this reason, the world is "all-body" and "all-form." And these are: one body, one soul, one world, one God. From whom the divine reason and the divine Word—most like a rushing river flowing from the heights into the depths with headlong speed—permeates all things, glides into all things, and flows into all things. And this divine reason and divine Word, what else is it, I ask, but Divine Wisdom, the architect of all? Which (as the wise man sings) <note>A likely reference to the Book of Wisdom 7:24: "For wisdom is more mobile than any motion."</note> is more mobile than all mobile things. And which, while being one, can do all things, and remaining in itself, renews all things. This is the first part.

                               Chapter                                  II
Heaven, therefore, the <term>sensible god</term> <note>original: *sensibilis deus*; the physical, visible cosmos as a manifestation of the divine</note>, is the administrator of all bodies, whose increases and decreases the sun and moon have received by lot. But of heaven itself, and of the soul itself, and of all things that are in the world, the Governor is He who is the Maker of all things, God. For from all the aforesaid things, of which He is the governor, a constant influx is carried through the world: through the soul of all kinds and all species, and through the nature of things. Moreover, the world has been prepared by God as a receptacle for all-encompassing species. Thus, shaping nature through species, He has led the world through the four elements all the way to heaven. All things are to be pleasing to the sight of God. All things hanging from above are divided into species in this way which I am about to say: The genera of all things follow their own species, so that the totality is the genus, and the species is a part of the genus. Therefore, there is the genus of gods, the genus of demons, and equally that of men, and similarly of birds. And all things that the world holds within itself generate species similar to themselves. There is also another genus of animal: a genus indeed without senses, yet not lacking a soul, whence it rejoices in benefits... <note>Hermes likely refers here to plants or "living" inanimate objects that react to their environment but lack perception.</note>

<summary>Hermes describes the universe as a singular, living organism animated by a "World Soul" and guided by a divine Word or Wisdom that flows through all matter. He explains the hierarchical relationship between God, the physical heavens, and the various categories (genera) of living beings, including gods, demons, and humans.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Divine Word, Logos, four elements, sensible god, taxonomy of beings, Hermeticism, divine influx</keywords>

...and is diminished and corrupted by adversities. I speak of all things that take root in the earth, the stalks and the plants. Through which, though they may die out individually by species, yet the fertility of being born <note>The inherent reproductive power of nature that ensures life continues even when individuals perish.</note> nonetheless remains constant.
